WOMAN'S WORI4),

'CHAT and GOSSIP.

(instry, for none of our competitors can beat us in production, except in the cheaper 'varieties.

The Return of the Pocket. Pockets are gradually coming into general use again; at present they are restricted to walking skirts and coats. On the former they are placed at the side, and stitched on the outside, just below the hips. They are quite ornamental ad- ditions to the skirt, and are often braided. The yoke skirt, gathered slightly at the back, with pleated skirt beneath, is a favourite design for renovations. The front and back are quite plain, with a broad box pleat, the fulness being at the sides. Sashes and belts vary very much, and some are exceedingly quaint. Many of the waistless dresses have a superfluity of drapery that enlarges the figure to a degree that is any- thing but graceful. One some models, narrow black silk ribbon takes the place of the usual sash. This is wound round and round the figure, like a soldier's puttee, extending from bust to hips, and ties in bows under the arm, the ends , falling thence to considerably below the waist.

The Popular Medici Collar and the New Choker. Tailor-made suits of the severe type are fur- nished with the old-fashioned stock collar, very similar to that worn by men in 1830, but less voluminous. A high linen collar that fastens at the back goes round the neck; below this a long stock of black or coloured satin is wound round twice, and tied .in a bow in front, the long ends falling half-way at least to the waist. It is a clumsy contrivance, and altogether in- artistic, but it may find favour on account of its novelty. Another choker collar, generally

_ 4,4 seen on plainly-made shirt blouses, of crepe de chine, silk, or delaine, is less cumbersome and much more practical, since it is innocent of wire, and made to turn back with a small or large V-shaped opening and revers, or to button up tightly, keeping the neck warm. The medici collar, which seems to suit all types—except of course, the woman whose heck is abnormally short—is still deservedly popular. It is high at the back and sides, and open in front.

A New Motor Veil. The veil is an important detail of the toilette, and to be effective must be chosen with great care. Shadow-late patterns in tulle are most effective, and a veil that is said to improve any complexion is made of chalk-white tulle, upon which irregularly spaced spots have been worked 1 in blue thread. Adjustment, of course, is a matter of vast importance, and it is an art many women fail to master. Many a dainty hat loses its chic because its wearer is incapable of arranging the veil worn with it, neatly and smartly. Very beautiful as works of art(are the Brussels lace veils shown this year, but some of the patterns on them are so heavy, and the designs are so close together, that they horribly disfigure the face behind them. Much discretion is necessary when making a choice. A new motor veil of the Yashmak type is emi- nently practical and cosy. The upper part that covers the face is of net, the lower of ninon. The latter goes round the neck, giving the warmth required when a collar hi gfi at the neck is worn, for then the cold wind circles round, to the discomfort of the motorist.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
By MARJORIE.

Homely Clubs for Women. The success which has attended the opening of the first Women's Cafe and Recreation Rooms at Hammersmith, has encouraged an extension of the movement to other parts of London. The scheme was inaugurated by Mrs. Juson Herr,' and the idea is to provide a meeting-place for women whose men folk are on active service, where they cart enjoy social intercourse, read papers, write letters, and be entertained on certain days of the week. A piano, comfort- able furniture, and necessary kitchen utensils are provided by friends interested in the move- ment, and tea and light refreshments can be obtained at cost price. Altogether it is a very practical effort to combat that evil of which we have heard so much lately—the resort of work- ing women to the public-houses—and one would be glad, to see the opening of these homely clubs for womenfolk in all the large industrial centres.

Women Toy Makers. • The Women's Emergency Corps is doing much that is useful in finding work for girls and women thrown out of employment by the war, and is making a very practical attempt to cap- ture the toy-making industry of Germany. The peasants who hive in and around the Black Forest, and make the cheap wooden toys that Germany sells to the whole world, work long hours for a mere- pittance, but there is no reason why the better class toys should not be produced in our own villages. It is proposed to send out skilled women instructors to various parts of the country, and where this already has been done, it is encouraging to find that women have rapidly become proficient in the work. Most of the old arts and crafts of the country districts have been killed by machinery, and in places not reached by the factory system there is much unemployed available labour, which only wants organisation and instruction to direct it into useful channels.

Galalith Buttons. British manufacturers should succeed in cap- turing a great part of the trade in foreign buttons, of which immense quantities are, in the ordinary way, imported from Germany and Austria. The linen button still remains a Brit- ish speciality, but foreign competition is very keen in the manufacture of most other kinds. A new substance obtained from milk, and called " galalith," has lately been invented on the Continent. It is very light, resembles horn, and vegetable ivory, and makes very handsome buttons. British manufacturers are now ex- perimenting with the hope of discovering the method of making galalith, and thus capturing some of the enemy's trade. Brass and gilt but- tons are made almost entirely i Germany, also those of steel, and all ornamental kinds used for blouses. Trouser buttons also come now from Germany. It is to be hoped that in future the public will support the British button in-

MY DEAR CHILDREN,— Soon after my last week's letter was in your hands our Enipire was plunged into mourn- ing through the death of that veteran soldier, Lord Roberts. , You will probably know that he died in France after inspecting the Indian troops, so that it is not necessary for me to go into details about his death. Instead I am collecting a few true stories about him, and these I hope to give you next week. He was a man to be proud of, and every Britisher will always remember " Bobs " as a true soldier and a true friend of soldiers. He died as he would have wished—whilst serv- ing the country he loved so well. Throughout his life he tried to help others, and this is what I have been trying to impress upon all my nephews and nieces. Last week I gave two examples of how the boys and girls from the Lancaster-street and Piekmere-street districts have done their share. Were there any of my young friends amongst them? If there were I would be pleased to hear from them, for I would like them to tell me how they made their arrangements. Last week I stated that I would announce a new competition to-day, and it will take the form of an essay on "How should I spend this Christmas?" The closing date will be Tues- day, December 15th. Your loving UNCLE NED.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
A TERRIBLE TOOTHACHE.

. If any of you should have a toothache—and it's very likely that you will—just look into the mirror and notice how small is the little white tormentor that is causing so much pain. Then —by way of comfort—reflect how much worse it would be if this tiny white thing were an elephant's great tusk, with toothache all through it. At Exeter Change, in London, there was, many years ago, a menagerie in the second story of the building., Here the elephant—" Chunee" by name—a very quiet, well-trained beast—was confined in a cage, under which the floor had been strengthened to support his weight. Chunee never came out, but seemed very happy. Suddenly he became raving mad, and screamed and trumpeted, and endeavoured vigorously to tear away the iron bars of his cote. Now, if he had succeeded in getting out upon the floor, Mr. Chunee would have immediately dropped through into an apothecary-shop below. If he had fallen into the scales, his exact weight might have been ascertained, after a fashion; but, in other respects, a mad elephant in a drugstore would have been far worse than a bull in a china-shop. If he had been sane, he might have had a nice time eating the licorice and cough lozenges and sugar-coated pills and candy, but as he wasn't sane the accident was not to be desired. _ _

Chunee -g-r-eNV-more and more wild and dan- gerous, until, at last, the "Beefeaters," who are the keepers of the Tower of London, were called upon to destroy the poor beast. They discharged many balls from their old-fashioned muskets into his body, but loss of blood seemed to increase his fury, and not lessen his strength. There were no rifle teams in those days to reach his brain with a single shot, so a piece of artillery was actually brought up, and poor Chunee, obeying his keeper's voice, even in his rage, kneel d down and was shot to death with a cannon ball. Now just think of what an awful thing six feet of toothache must have been, and pity poor Chunee!

THE WELL-BELOVED. (By TEssig SINN.) Thou Well-beloved, a cause is won By thy strong service given. Death's silent barque now drifteth Towards the shore of Heaven. " Bobs!" rest in peace. Thy duet is dear ; We lay thee 'mid the great: Falls on thy tomb a splendid tear, From Empire as from State.

But who shall follow in thy train, From out the eternal throng? None from thy dust shall rise, again To vanquish Empire wrong! Sleep on. The mighty task of earth Was well performed by thee: Then wake, in higher realms, to birth, And lead to victory.

SPIRITUALISM'S ANTIQUITY.

Lecture on a Cult that is as "Old as the Hills."

" We owe a greater debt to Spiritualism than many of us think!" To justify this statement of his, Mr. Walter Howell, of Birmingham, delivered an instructive lecture at the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening. Councillor E. Osborne presided over a good attendance.

Mr. W. E. Stout, president of the Warrington Spiritualist Society, said that his reason for inviting Councillor Osborne to preside was that it had been thought desirable to have a chair- man who, whilst outside their movement, was sympathetically disposed towards it. He would like to point out a significant statement made on Sunday by a local Nonconformist minister, in reference to the death of Lord Roberts: "We know that, although he is dead, his spirit still lives; and that he will be able to carry on his work more effectively now than if he had been alive !"

===== 0002601_19141121_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY HE WAS IN THE CHAIR.

The Chairman said he would like to explain why he was in the chair. It was his desire that people should listen to lectures even if they did not agree with what the lecturer had to tell them. He had a keen desire for know- ledge. They were apt to be perturbed, at pre- sent, about things; but, if they would attend more meetings similar to that one, they would be helped to combat those evil influences which were disturbing their minds and thoughts. Whilst some of them were concerned about the municipal debt, that was not their only debt; and he was afraid that many of them spent their time considering only mundane matters, and neglected the spiritual ones. They had to realise that they were not bodies alone, nor spirits alone, but both. Whilst their bodies isolated them, their spirits united them. What- ever opponents might say in criticism, if they would only grasp their own personal situations and desires and attributes, and if they would only realise that they had a divinity in them, which could be presented in their own homes, he was satisfied that the citizenship of their town would be nobler, better and purer. (Ap- plause.) All were critics, and the most ignor- ant man was very often the keenest critic! He could not, however, stand those people who never said anything, and pretended they were clever; he would rather they spoke, even if they made mistakes. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141121_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
SPIRITUALISM AS OLD AS THE HILLS.

The Lecturer said he did not want anybody to think that the fundamental principles of Spirit- ualism were modern, for they were as old as the hills. If they could say that God was a spirit; then all His manifestations were spiritual phenomena; and they must realise themselves in a world that was infinite in an infinite variety of ways. Spiritualism had been there long before the revival of modern Spiritualism, but for some centuries it was not spoken about very much, because it was regarded as an offence against the individuals concerned. They owed many debts to modern Spiritual- ism. It had, for one thing, converted millions of people who would otherwise have remained in doubt as to man's possible survival of death, and would therefore have come to fear death; and it gave those who remained strength in the ordeal of bereavement. It had also stimulated scientific research, and had led to the discovery of many things. It had done much towards the emancipation of negro-slavery. History had been vindicated from Genesis to Revelation by it, whilst mental healing had done untold good through it. (Applause.) The Chairman, in thanking Mr. Howell for his lecture, said much as man knew, there was a great deal beyond his ken. Our trying to criticise the acts of God was like an ant trying to see things as man sees them.

A formal vote of thanks was passed to the Lecturer and the Chairman, on the motion of Mr. W. E. Stout.

Little Girl's Terrible Fate. Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Police Buildings on Monday afternoon concerning the death of Catherine Kelly, aged three years, who resided with her Parents at 93 Athol-street Earlestown. From the evidence Of the mother 'it appeared that she was bringing in clothing from the yard on TlilirsdaY of last week when she saw the child on the sofa with her clothing on fire. She called for assistance and the child was taken to the Warrington Workhouse Infirmary, where death p.m. on Friday. The fire- d occurred d moved from its place, and there ogcucaurra t been eight was burnt paper on the floor.—The doctor's re- port stated that the child was suffering from "extensive burns" when admitted.—A verdict of " Accidental death" was returned.

IN THE COURTS. BOROUGH POLICE COURT

MONDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst and Bolton, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. S. Wright, W. Furness, W. Roberts, G. Pilling and T. Locker. A " F ishy " Story.—John Towey, 3, Gas-street, was charged with having been found on the enclosed premises of a warehouse in St. Aus- tin's-lane.—The Chief Constable said that Mr. McCabe, the owner of the Warehouse, went to it at 6.40 p.m. on Saturday and discovered that the padlock had been broken. Prisoner was inside and pretended to be asleep. Mr. McCabe secured the door and went for P.C. Taylor. When they got to the warehouse again pris- oner had decamped with about one shillings- worth of fish, having effected his escape by breaking a window. When arrested prisoner tried to make out that it was a drunken freak. —Prisoner: I fell through the door and I did not know where I was, so I broke the window to get out.—The Chief: And the fish walked down his throat, your Worships, without assist- ance. (Laughter.)—Towey was committed to prison for a month.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Messrs. Hay- ward, C. Jackson Holmes, and W. T. Williams. Said She Was "An Old Customer."—Sarah Rawlinson, a married woman, of 12, Morris- court, was charged with obtaining by false pre- tences, on November 11th, two pairs of boots, valued at 14s. 6d., the property of Thomas Broadhurst, boot dealer, of 76, Mersey-street.— Superintendent Williamson asked for a remand, stating that the woman went into the shop whilst Mr. Broadhurst was out, representing that she was an old customer; and obtained, from the assistant the boots in question. These were afterwards pledged at two pawnshops. It was subsequently found that the woman was not known at all to Mr. Broadhurst. —The re- mand was granted.

DOCTORS AND HEALTH.

Fish and Chips, Pheasant and

Cham- Pagne.

Health topics seem to have a great fascination for Adult Schools. _ _

Dr. Manson's lecturette to the Stockton Heath School attracted a large and interested audi- ence. The Doctor began by traversing the doc- trine of Mr. Chesterton, that the ordinary medical man knows little about health, because he is constantly in an atmosphere of disease. Statistics had been produced in years gone by to show how far down the doctors came in the list of occupational mortality. This seemed to show that the medical man had not much idea of keeping his own health. The public could turn round and say, "Physician, heal thyself!" On the other hand, there were other statistics to show that the infantile death-rate in doc- tors' families was extraordinarily low, indicat- ing that at least medical men could look after their own offspring with remarkable care. Chesterton's idea seemed to be that the jour- nalist was a safer guide to health than the doctor. But unfortunately the journalist had not even the few facts which usually sufficed for his professional work. Dr. Manson pointed out how almost all the great religions of the world had tried to take the question of health under their wing. From the Ganges, the sacred cleansing river, to the miracles of Lourdes and Holywell, there were evidences of the interest shown by religion in health. It was important to remember that health was not an end in itself, but a means to an end. As a famous English judge had said, "Doctors are the men who help people to carry on their work." It was quire posdible for people to think too much about their own health. Sometinns it became an obsession. Hence arose those modern mock-religions, based upon a false philosophy. Questions in quick succession showed how keen the members were to receive further en- lightenment. Food reform, inoculation, sani- tation, and various methods of burial came up for discussion. As illustrating the modern divorce from Nature, which has come about through industrialism, the lecturer mentioned .the case of an old woman, who now lives on fish and chips from a shop, who well remembers how some kind of porridge used to be the staple breakfast dish in most working class homes. He was careful to point out that the condemna- tion of fish and chips ought to extend to the pheasant and champagne dinners, which often became the life standard in other strata of humanity.

PHILOMATIIS AND CONSCRIPTION

Ridicule for " Saturday Afternoon Soldiering." CASE FOR VOLUNTARISM.

"Should conscription be adopted in this country?" This was the subject of a debate held under the auspices of the Philomathic Society in their rooms on Wednesday evening. The affirmative view was taken by Mr. C. S. Woods and the negative by Mr. H. Dutton. Mr. N. Nield (the president) presided over a good attendance.

OUR UNPREPARED "TERRIERS." In his opening Mr. Woods said it was his intention to go as fully 'as he could into the question of the Territorial Force and explain how inadequate it was for the purpose for which it was formed. When the force was created it was supposed to consist of 315,000 men, but the actual numbers were only 265,000 in 1913. Almost half of the force had not qualified even in the preparatory stage of rifle shooting. It was ludicrous to think that we had been plac- ing the defence of our shores into the hands of men who had not fired 100 rounds of aminuni- tion. Then again the small amount of train- ing given to the men did net tend to bring about discipline. The whole thing had savoured too much of Saturday afternoon soldiering. Now that war had broken out the force was under- going training, instead of. being an efficient force ready to repel any invasion which might have taken place. Supposing the rising in Egypt had come off, the Indian Army could not have left India, and our own men would have had to go and leave England at the mercy of the enemy, for our only me-alia of defence in addition to the Navy would be .the Territorial Force, which had just started training. One of the arguments against his idea was that we could not be invaded, but supposing the, Ger- man fleet did away with our fleet, they Would either blockade or invade. He was of the opinion that invasion would be fTie more likely. In order to deal effectively with an,invasion we ought to have a proper army, fully equipped, and, under a voluntary system, we had no such army. • Of course, it had been saTil that if an invasion took place the nation would spring to arms. It would be a bad thing to do, because there would be none of the discipline and energy which was absolutely essential to suc- cess, and there would be none of the necessary training. The courage of the nation would only hasten its destruction. The men in the worthy and excellent training corps which had ' recently sprung up would acknowledge that if they were against trained men it would be a case of butchery pure and simple. The advan- tages to a nation with compulsory service were immense—physically, morally and socially. The cost of such an army would be about 100 per cent, more than the cost of the present army. but there would be a diminution in the naval .expenditure. The expenditure on the present army was out of propiirtion to the results achieved. Compulsory service would be of great advantage to the country all round. (Ap- plause.)

~•

INVASION MORE REMOTE THAN EVER. Mr. Dutton said the burden of Mr. Woods' argument seemed to be based on the supposition that if our fleet were defeated. Germany would be able to land, say, 70,000 men. If the 'fleet were beaten neither conscription nor the Ter- ritorial Force could save us. That would never happen, but supposing it did, the German lines of communicition would be cut off, and it would be practically suicidal for them to try it, A permanent system of con- scription was unnecessary in this coun- try. "The Day" had now come; • ' our fleet had swept the seas, as was expected, and the much-talked-of invasion was further away than ever. We had proved recently that one volunteer was worth ten pressed men. The present war was one against excessive militar- ism, from which the German people would be glad to be freed. Those who advocated conscrip- tion were running contrary to the essential characteristics of the country: Conscription bred the spirits of servility and submission, which were mis-called obedience and punctual- ity. Such a spirit was incompatible with the watchword of liberty. Conscription would double our expenditpxe for national defence. It was argued that it would break down class distinctions, but they all knew of the arrogance of the German officers. Zabern was well within their recollection. Conscription, considered as a means of elevating the social, physical and moral sides of life, was not to be trusted. Phy- sical benefits could be obtained by reforms, which the vast expenditure that conscription would necessitate would render impossible. As a means of breaking down class distinction, conscription was doubtful, in view of the aris- tocracy and plutocracy in the country; and, , again, it would render wars of aggression more likely.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
A BRITISH COMPROMISE.

Mr. F. Chadwick said he believed that a form of conscription on common-sense lines would meet our requirements. Dr. Manson said that instead of voting for one side or the other, they should all be British and vote for a compromise. The degrees of voluntary service were things to be considered, for at the present time there were men who did not join out of a pure spirit of patriotism, but in many cases were forced to join by various reasons. A spirit of compromise, whereby every man could be called upon to give some service to the country would be advantageous. Mr. H. Sherburn argued that the men in the Army and in the Territorials were not ade- quately dealt with as regards payment: He was of the opinion that unless they exhausted all the possibilities of the voluntory system, con- scription should be kept in the background.

SATISFACTORY VOLUNTARY SYSTEM. Mr. Rathbone said that so far as he could see it was not economically sound nor expedient to introduce conscription into this country. All military expenditure was recognised as unpro- ductive, and under conscription the men would be taken from productive employment and made non-producers. Therefore, the wealth of the country would be reduced and taxation in- creased. People had argued, "If you will have peace, prepare for war." Every nation. in Europe had been preparing for war, and now there was the greatest war in history taking place. The present voluntary system was quite satisfactory, for we had the largest overseas army in the world. A splendid example of the efficacy of the voluntary system was to be seen at the present time. _ _ _ _ _

lEr. P. S. Dunn said that the conflict had come about because we were not prepared for war and Germany was. If we had had a system .4f conscription there would not have been any war.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSCRIPTION ON SWISS LINES AS AN

ALTERNATIVE.

Dr. J. Bamforth said that if we had a system of Imperialism we should not need to go on in- creasing armaments. _ .

The Chairman expressed himself in favour of a compromise, and agreed with Dr. Bamforth.l He was of the opinion, however, that so far as the defence of the country was concerned, there was no sensible argument to be brought against conscriptiom on the lines adopted in Switzerland.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VOTING.

In reply to the various speakers, Mr. Dutton said that Germany had absolutely made up her miud to dominate Europe, and conscription might have delayed the present war, but could never have prevented it. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Woods said that men were now rolling up behind their time. They ought to have been prepared before. Those who were against con- scription did not say what they would do, but stood by without doing anything. On a vote being taken the majority agreed with Mr. Woods. Several members, however, abstained from voting on the direct question.

Like Poetry.

A reporter asked John J. Hayes, the winner of the Marathon race, if long distance running was difficult.

Running, said the young man, "is a good deal like writing poetry. And you know what Victor Hugo said abolit that."

"No. What did he say?" asked the reporter. " A visitor," said Mr. Hayes, "asked Hugo if it was hard to make good poetry. "'Hard?' said Hugo, with a laugh. 'Hard? Bless me, no. It is either quite easy or—im- possible.' "

===== 0002601_19141121_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
PRESBYTERIAN DIAMOND JUBILEE

Sixtieth Anniversary Services Continued

AT ST. JOHN'S CHURCH.

Diamond jubilee celebration services in con- nection with St. John's Presbyterian Church— the first of which were held the previous week -L-were continued on Sunday, when the special preacher for the day was the Rev. Wm. McNeill, of Birkenhead (and late of Dublin), and when large congregations attended each service. In the morning the preacher look as his text Luke xxi. 19: " In your patience possess ye your souls."

===== 0002601_19141121_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
EVERY MAN'S DIVINE HUNGER.

This text, said the preacher, gave at the same time both a precept and a promise. For the disciples there was a life of anguish and trial, but God kept watch over His own. _ _ _

_ They could gather their Lord's general teach- ing as to the soul, the existence of which He assumed. In all men there was a Divine hunger for purity and peace. Jesus knew this, and appealed to it. Every man had, and was, a soul; when he was stripped of all the circum- stances that had gathered around him during his progress through life, there remained the man himself—his soul. That was what a man was worth, no more and no less. And Jesus said quite distinctly that it was possible for a man to lose his soul, through carelessness and sin and easy-going indifference. There was also the opposite and corresponding truth—a man could win his soul!

===== 0002601_19141121_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
CONVERSION AND SALVATION.

Now the winning of the soul took time; it was k progressive affair. It had been said that con- version and salvation were the same thing; but the former, though the first and deciding step. Was not salvation. It started the winning of the soul, and that Meant a great deal; but it took time to win the soul—probably it took eternity! How fared it with them in that business of soul-winning? When the shock of death came, would it find in them something that death could not destroy? He supposed there was no reason why a man's soul should not thrive in Prosperity, -sunshine and happiness. The.char- acters of some people were so thankful and generous that no prosperity could alter them. But, by most, the soul was won through trial and by patience; and had they made as much of their trials as they might have done?

Then, welcome each rebuff That turns earth's smoothness rough, Each sting that bids not sit Nor stand, but go! Be our joys three parts pain! Strive, and hold cheap the strain; Learn, nor account the pang; dare, • Never grudge the throe! The winning of the soul was a matter of trial.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SOUL OF THE NATION.

This, true of individuals, was true also of nations; for nations had souls, which could be lost and won. They would agree that, during the past years of prosperity, all had not been well with their country—she had achieved material things and forgotten spiritual ones. Who had not trembled for the soul of England? Now the whole nation was passing under a great wave of anxiety, and in that trial life's values would undergo a change. They would see the worth of the pure; they would be encouraged to take up their Lord's teirching—and not only to profess it, but to live up to it. Chastened and humbled, they would emerge victorious—in that England had won, not a great war, but a great soul, which, but for that trial, she might have lost!

===== 0002601_19141121_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
STRENGTH THROUGH SUFFERING

"Our Victory Is The Victory God Wants."

In the evening the reverend gentleman took for his text, '" Father, if Thou be willing, re- move this cup from Me; nevertheless, not My Will, but Thine, be done.' And there appeared an angel unto Him from heaven strengthening Him," St. Luke xxii. 42, 4.3. Those words were precious to them, said the preacher, for they assured them that our Lord's prayer was not unanswered. Although the cup was not re- moved, He was made strong. There were many people who found Christ through suffering, and it was there, in the Garden of Gethsemane, that they met Him when He was most human. In these days of trial people were drawing nearer to Christ through suffering. Perhaps in the modern stress and strain they would find affinities with Christ which they never ex- pected. Perhaps a look of recognition would pass between them and Christ at last ! He (the preacher) had no doubt but, that during the trials and anguishing days of the war, a large number of iken and women would come to know Christ ..an the felfowship of His suffering.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
PRAYER LIKE A BOAT HOOK.

----• ••--• Dealing with the power of prayer, the preacher said that every prayer was bpunded by submis- sion. The beginner's idea of "prayer was that of the child, "Ask for whatever you want. -Try to bring God's will down to your own !" Prayer, however, was like a boat-hook. They did not use a boat-hook to draw the land to the boat, but to draw the boat to the land. Prayer was man's boat-hook. He could not use it to draw God's will down to his, but to draw his will up to God. They should prdy for what was right, and in connection with the war pray for the victory of the Allies; the triumph of right- eousness and peace. Their prayers would be answered, becausb the victory they wanted was the victory God wanted. They ought to be en- couraged to look for, and expect, indirect an- swers to prayer. God did not take the cup from His Son, but sent an angel to strengthen Him. They had all been praying for a prosperous issue of the war, and, because the end was still far out of sight, some people were speaking as though their prayers had not been answered. That was a great mistake. He knew scores of men and women who had gone from a prayer meeting, or private devotion, carrying a burden of anxiety, but who had gone with brave step, steeled to quiet indifference, to patience, and to steadfastness, which had not been the case with them before. Was not the courage and pluck which the men and women of the country were showing an answer to prayer? That was the angel from heaven come to strengthen them. They had asked.for victory and trusted to have it in God's good time, but, in, the meantime, they must not forget that God had sent them strength_ in this time of stress and strife.

Special hymns and psalms were sung by the choir, under the leadership of Mr. A. G. Lewsey, organist, whilst at the evening service the Misses Mounfield and Smith rendered the duet, "Our Blessed Redeemer" (Mee Pattison). The anthem, "Lift up your heads, 0 ye gates !" (Hopkins) was rendered in a creditable manner by the choir.

TO-MORROW'S COMMEMORATIVE SERVICES. I The special services commemorative of the Act which sixty years ago made St. John's an integral part of revived Presbyterianism in England will be brought to a close to-morrow (Sunday), when the minister of the congrega- tion (the Rev. William Reid) will occupy the pulpit, taking for a morning subject "The Vision of Habakkuk," while in the evening the I topic of the occasion, "The Year of Jubilee," will find a prominent place. Large congrega- tions waited upon the ministry of Dr. Monro Gibson and the Rev.. William McNeill on the past two Sundays, and it is pretty certain that to-morrow's services will prove no less a fea- ture in the special season. The congregation at the commencement set itself to the high ideal of uniting with the celebration of this sixtieth birthday the realisation of a £6O offering, and rejoice in having so far reached within .tl5 of their goal. The minister and office-bearers are very anxious it should be known that a most cordial welcome will be extended to all with any past connection, or present sympathy, with the con- gregation, who are able tiO attend the social on Thursday evening of nest week.

"AFTER 11111 WAR."

Christadelphian Interpretation of Biblical Prophecy.

The third lecture in the "After the War" series was delivered at the Christadelphian Meeting Room, Bold-street, on Monday evening, when Mr. A. S. Wadsworth, of Keighley, spoke upon "The Kingdom of God." The outline of the lecture was "a divine, political kingdom to be established upon the earth, with Christ as King, sitting upon the throne of David." Mr. Joseph P. Jennings, who presided 'over a good attendance, read, by way of introduction, Matthew vi.-7-the second portion of the Sermon on the Mount. GOD'S APPROACHING GLORY.

The Lecturer said that there were kingdoms upon the earth, at present, which were very evidently not divine. Man had come to see that the world was not ruled by wisdom, Mit by greed. In .Revelation iv. 11, it was said: " Thou art worthy, 0 Lord, to receive glory and honour and power, for Thou hait created all things, and for Thy pleasure they are and were created." Had God found pleasure in the things they saw? No, but He had made it clear that the earth shall be filled with His glory, and that there should be. " peace and good-will toward men." That was what Jesus had been born for.

The term "the Kingdom of God" had been, greatly• misunderstood—men said it was either a state of the heart, or a state a felicity some- where beyond the skies. But what was it? They had been told to pray: "Thy Kingdom come; thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven." The divine kingdom was to be estab- lished on earth—" Thine, 0 Lord, is . . . all that is in the heavep and in the earth . . . thine is the kingdom . . . and thou art exalted as head above all" (1 Chron. xxix. 11). In 2 Chron. ix. it was acknowledged by the other nations that Israel was the chosen early land: "Happy are thy men, and . . thy servants, which stand continually before thee and hear thy wisdom. Blessed be the Lord thy God, which . . . set thee on His throne, to be king for the Lord thy God: because thy God loved Israel, to establish them `for ever, . . to do judgment and justice." Even after the children of Israel had been permitted to enter the land, God reserved it for Himself—" The land shall. not be sold for ever: for the land is Mine; for ye are strangers and sojourners with Me" (Leviticus xxv. 23). ISRAEL'S DESTINY. —•-

Besides providing the land for His chosen people (Deuteronomy xiv. 2—" Thou art an holy people unto the Lord thy God, and the Lord hath chosen thee to be a peculiar people unto Himself, above all the nations that are upon the earth") God had nominated their future king. "When thou art come unto the land which the Lord thy God giveth thee," ran Deuteronomy xvii. 14-15, ". . and shalt possess it . . thou shalt set him king over thee whom the Lord thy God shalt choose." God also gave them a law, as shown in Deuteronomy xxxi. 9: "And Moses wrote this law, and delivered it unto the priests the sons of Levi, which bare the ark of the covenant of the Lord, and unto all the elders of Israel." In chapter xvii. of the same book, He gave Israel many instruc- tions as to their laws. As to the foundation of God's earthly kingdom, it was stated that "it shall come to pass, when thy days be expired, that thou must go to be with thy fathers, that I will raise up thy seed after thee, which shall be of thy sons; and I will establish his king- dom" (1 Chron. xvii. 11). \

===== 0002601_19141121_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
"KING OF THE JEWS."

Who was to rule over Israel in the coming days? Luke i. 30 told them that "the angel said unto her, Fear not, Mary: for thou bast found favour with God,'" and continued: "Thou shalt bring forth a son . . Jesus, . . . and the Lord God shall give unto him the throne of his father David: and he shall reign over the house of Jacob for ever; and of his kingdom there shall be no end."

===== 0002601_19141121_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR GOD'S PUNISHMENT.

Nation Must Turn From Luxury and __ Pleasure-Loving.

-The annual meeting of the Warrington branch of the English Chuich Union was held in Fairfield Schools on Saturday afternoon. The Rev. W. J. Stanton presided, and he was accompanied on the platform by the Rev. J. 11. G. Hollingworth (secretary).--The Chairman was re-elected. President of the Warrington branch and of the South-West District Union.—ln ack- nowledgment the reverend gentleman said they required a large number of members. The following officials for the Warrington branch were also elected :—Vice-chairman and treasurer, Mr. C. S. Burgess; secretary, Rev. J. H. Cii.•Hollingworth; agent, Rev. J. Bell Cox; committee, Rev. J. Holden, Mr. E. Hall Coates, Mr. F. M. Darby, and Miss White. PROGRESS MAINTAINED LOCALLY.

The Secretary presented the Treasurer's- re- port, which showed that the year was com- menced with a sum of lie. 11d, and that there was now .13 Is. 4d. in hand.—ln his own report as secretary, the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth said that progress had been maintained during the past year. They had now a membership of 55, and there had been no withdrawals. .THE WAR IN THE LIGHT OF RELIGION. In the course of his address, the President appealed for more members, and pointed out the aims and objects of the society. Referring to the war, he said be thought they ought to examine the present struggle in the light of religion. It had tried the faith of many people, who argued "How is it God can allow such horrors now?" and "Why does He not step in and prevent such horrors?" or "How can He be a just and merciful God to allow these things?" He (the speaker) was of the opinion that, instead of trying their faith, the war ought to make their faith stronger, because it was only another instance of the way in which God had always worked. Because they had been enjoying a large mea- sure of prosperity the people had become luxury and pleasure-loving and had disregarded God's commandments. The war was God's punish- ment. The people must turn away from being excessively pleasure-loving and resolve to he better iu the future. The war had been sent in mercy, for they had been prevented from striving amongst themselves and had to join to- gether in fighting a common enemy, who had yielded to ideas which were heathen and not Christian. When the war was over they would realise Christ more fully, to their own advan- tage.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
VICAR AND _ %VAR-TIME MERRY-

MAKING.

People Who Are Ready_ To "Dance

Over the New-made Graves."

_ Writing in the current issue of his Parish Magazine, the Vicar of Burtonwood the Rev. A. M. Mitchell) says : " Our people, the greater number of them, still need to be reminded of the Sunday and week-day intercessions on behalf of our soldiers and sailors exposed to so many and great dan- gers. How does it come to pass that there is so much indifference,, callous indifference in our midst: that our people sit 'down to eat, rise up to play with, an unconcern indicative of a dis- tressing heartlessness? Is it a time for dancing, whist drives, drinking, and miscalled merry- making? How can people find pleasure and sat- isfaction in amusements, frivolity, and even drunkenness at a time when they should con- sider their ways, humiliate themselves by fast,. big and self-denial, falling low upon their knees before the Almighty? We confess to a sense of tteep shame and intense pain at the selfish, heartless attitude of the greater number of our people at this time of unique distress and awful chastisement. May God be merciful to those who are ready to dance over the new- made graves a our Tommies,' to hold high revel in the midst of death, to throw the dice and play cards on the tombstones of the slain !"

seats, the room is admirably adapted to its temporary purpose. At one end is a long table, and behind it sit the clerks whose business it is to dip their hands into the tempting array of gold and silver coins with which the drawers are laden and single out therefrom the sums specified on the "ring papers" of the married women and the maintenance warrants of the mothers whose sons have made them allocations of their pay. The business of paying-out is con- ducted with expedition and courtesy—such courtesy as is due to the dependents of the heroes-all who are fighting so minfully the cause of us stay-at-home admirers. Maybe the busy clerk finds time for a pleasantry that sends a ripple of laughter among the waiting women, joke begetting joke, and the wives and mothers, the precious coins gripped tightly in their closed hands, pass out into the street once more with smile-Wreathed faces. One looked in vain for that harshness, that want of tact and com- mon kindliness that one had heard of—from those who, obviously, don't know! A Cbnclusive Disclaimer.

It was alleged at the Trades Council meet- ing that women had been told with brutal bluntness, when they had presented their "ring. papers," "Can't pay that; he's dead !" where- upon, not unnaturally, if it were true, wives and mothers had fainted or had broken down in pitiful fashion. But, as Mark Twain once said, when reading obituary notices concerning him- self in the American Press, "The story is grossly exaggerated !" As a matter of fact, the woman unfortunate enough to lose her husband or her son-support would be made acquainted with the fact before the Post Office officials; indeed the only intimation that they get is that Private So-and-so's pay-book is re-called. For all they know the book may be required by the Army Pay Office for the adjustment of some clerical errors—several such cases have occurred, and it has been necessary to .tell the wife or mother that further payment could not be made until the book is sent back from headquarters. leeVerything is all right when the book comes back, not only is the current week's pay handed over, but 'all arrears are settled up. "We could not have been guilty of uttering that heartless He's dead!' phrase," one of the officials assured our representative, "for the simple reason that the woman would be in- formed of that sad fact from the War Office; we never get the intimation. That settles the story about women fainting when we have told them that their husbands or sons have been killed. We don't do the telling; it's not our de- partment. The War Office see to that! On the first day we started paying out a woman did faint, but that was not due to any information we conveyed, but to the exceptional rush and the pressure of other women round about her. We hadn't got the Parr Hall as a waiting-room then."

Our informant added that there were eases in which the husband was reported as having been killed, but the wives still went on drawing the Army separation allowance, because the P.O. had had no authority from headquarters to cease paying it. A case in point was that of a poor womaa who had that day drawn her fourth week's pay since she lost her bread- winner. £6OO A Week Pahl Out.

"It is absolutely untrue to say that we have occasioned a shock to any wife or mother," reiterated the official, by way of conclusion, ".because we do not know when a man is killed. All we tlo know is that the pay-book is re-called, and, so far as we are aware, the re-calling of it may be simply for the purpose of putting it right clerically !" Not all of the women come for their money on the Monday; some attend the next day, when the routine is precisely the same, and every effort is made to reduce the discomforts of the some- times necessarily long waits to a minimum. (Mem. : If somebody, or bodies, want to do the fighting man's loved ones a good turn, what about arranging for a cup of tea and a biscuit or two; it would be vastly appreciated!) The hours during which the Post Office is open for the purpose of payments to soldiers' dependents are from 8 to 8, and the busiest time is between 9.30 and 11.30. Altogether between 800 and 900 separation allowances are paid out, the total involved being over ,k600.'

Tempting. Property insured in London is valued at :e1,320,000,000, according to an announcement made by Lord Peel last week. One can almost hear the Kaiser smacking his lips.—" Punch."

MAYOR'S SUNDAY.

Great Civic and Patriotic Demonstration.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
CANON WILLIS AND OUR JUST WAR.

• As befitted the times, there was an air of the martial allied to the civio about the Mayoral churchgoing on Sunday. The presence in the procession of the serried ranks of those who have offered themselves for home service with the local Preparatory Training Corps supplied the main suggestion, and the music of the bands —the soul-inspiring national airs of the allied combatants, who, side by side, are fighting the common enemy, blending with the strains of the popular songs which Tommy Atkins has adopted-as his secular and spirit-raising war chants, and being punctuated, ever and anon, by the piercing shrill of bugle• calls and the ear-assailing throbs of the kettle-drum— heightened it. All the way from the Town Hall—where the procession was marshalled—to the distant Par- ish Church—huge crowds of people thronged the pavements, whilst yet more followed in the wake of the civic cavalcade.

Constantinople, and at the moment, there was special significance in the fact.

A CHRISTIANISED CONSTANTINOPLE—? They would doubtless recall—those of them who had dipped into the pages of history—that in the third century—three hundred years after Christ—the seat of the then greatest Empire was removed from Rome to the place that was now known as Constantinople, by Constantine the Great. A thousand years later—in the year 1453 to be precise—Constantinople Was taken by the Turks, and the Mohammedans settled there, with the result that for 460 years that great city, and the country of which it formed the capital, had been the seat of Molammedan worship. Now, as they knew, the Turks had joined arms with Germany, and by so doing, had, according to the Prime Min- ister, sealed their own doom, with the result that there was good cause for hoping and be- lieving that, when the present terrible war came to an end, Constantinople would again become the seat of a Christian Empire. Yes, verily the words of the text were the cry of the Church, and let them pray to God that the Anglican Communion, the Eastern Commitnion, and the great Roman Communion might again become one. Coming to the third aspect of the text, let them consider the German claim—the German: cry! Time was when Germany was Christian.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROCESSION.

A touch of sunshine would have added to the amenities, but, perhaps, after all, the sombre skies were more in accord with the feelings of the assembled processionists as the news went round that Britain's grand old warrior, Lord Roberts, had passed away. Crosfield's Band, under the conductorship of Mr. J. C. Taylor, heralded the approach of the long queue as it proceeded from the Park along Sankey-street, Buttermarket-street, and Church-street. Imme- diately behind came the members of the War- rington and District Preparatory Training Corps, commanded by Sergt.-Major Sullivan. They marched as men who had been accustomed to military discipline for years, instead of only a matter of a few brief weeks. The local Cadet Battalion of the Church Lads' Brigade contri- buted about 80 members to the procession, and they were in charge of Major Brooks, Capt. Bunch, Lieut. Morris, and Acting-Sergt.-Major S. Essenhigh. Their bugle band, like that of the Boy Scouts, who followed, added to the volume of martial strains which saluted the ear as the procession wended its way along the streets. The following troops of local Scouts were represented: Ist Warrington (in charge of Assistant-Scoutmaster Cartwright); 2nd War- rington (Scoutmaster Young)r and Ist Grappen- hall (Scoutmaster T. A. Edwards). Mr. J. B. Starkey (chairman of the local Boy Scouts' Association) walked with the troops. A detach- ment of ambulance men were in charge of Supt. S. Baldry. The Borough Fire Brigade (under the com- mand of Supt. Turner) preceded a detachment of the local police force, who were in charge of Mr. Martin Nicholls (the chief constable). The mace and sword bearers, in their quaint gowns, were followed by the Mayor, who was accom- Panied by the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway), and on returning from churcli also by the Rev. Canon Willis (his chaplain). The Deputy-Mayor (Dr. Joseph) and Mr. A. H. Cros- field followed, and then came members of the various local bodies.

The Town Council was represented by Alder- men Bennett, Tinnion and James Evans, and Councillors Barton, Chantler, Chrimes, Craik, Cunnington, Dickenson, Gough, Halliwell, Haw- thorn, Henshall, jAshford, Locker, Osborne, Shaw, Skelland, and Wilson. Others figuring in the procession were:— Corporation ocials Messrs. T. H. Spinks (assistant town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), A. M. Irer (borough surveyor), F. G. Ruddock (analyst), W. T. Flood (chief inspector of nuisances), E. Place (markets inspector), R. Whitfield (head gardener), W. S. Haddock (gas works manager), J. Gray (water engineer), and W. S. Howard (cashier of the gas department). Magistrates: Messrs. J. Bailey, W. Furness, A. J. Jolley, T. Locker and G. Pilling. Education Committee: Messrs. G. Walker, J. Moore Murray (director of education), and G. Pendlebnry (school of art). Warrington Infirmary: Messrs. J. H. Smeth- urst (chairman of the Board of Management), H. Collin, W. Daintith, T. C. Wadsworth, and J. H. J. Hampson (secretary), together, with a number of wounded soldiers who are under treatment in the institution. P.S.A. Societies: Messrs. W. Clegg and B. Farrar. Insurance Committee: Messrs. S. Brockle- hurst. W. Bibby, P. Delaney, F. Dickens, J. Elwell, J. Seed, and A. Tilling (clerk). The Friendly Societies Walked in the follow- ing order, in charge of Mr. T. P. Bradshaw, the chief marshal: R.A.O. Buffaloes (accompanied by their band under the conductorship of Mr. R. Smith), Hearts of Oak, National Independ- ent Order of Oddfellows, Irish National Fores- ters and Hibernians, Loyal Ancient Order of Shepherds, Independent Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity), Rational Association, An- cient Order of Druids, Free Gardeners, Catho- lic Grand Friendly United Order of. Oddfellows. Society, Ancient Order of Fores- ters,

===== 0002601_19141121_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE CHURCH. "If God Be With Us, Who Can Be

Against Us?"

Inside the church every available seat was occupied, and not a few of the worshippers found no seats .at all. The majority of the pews, both on the floor and in the commodious galleries, were reserved for those who had marched with the Mayor's entourage. His Worship, attended by his deputy (Councillor Joseph), the Assistant Town Clerk (Mr. Halls- way), and Mr. Arthur H. Crosfield, was accom- modated in a front pew, the mace and sword, which symbolise the civic state of Warrington, being borne before him up the aisle and placed in specially prepared brackets by his side..

The service was of a most impressive char- acter. As the processionists filed into their seats the organist (Mr. Darby) played the National Anthems of the Allies. The anthem, "Judge me, 0 Lord "—a specimen of Mozart in his most inspired vein—was beautifully ren- dered, both as to the delightful quartet-prelude and the grand massibg of the voices which leads to the glorious finale. The hymns selected were "0 God, our help in ages past," and "Stand up !—stand up for Jesus," the former being sung to a new tune, which lacks the rol- ling sonority of the older and more popular setting. At the close, after an offertory had been taken on behalf of the local Belgian Relief Fund, the congregation joined in singing the National Anthem, after which the proces- sion was re-formed and marched back to the Town Hall, where once again "God save the King" was sung before the crowd melted away.

EAST IS EAST AND WEST IS WEST-WILL

===== 0002601_19141121_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
EVER THE TWAIN MEET?

The sermon in the Parish Church was preached by the Mayor's Chaplain ' (the Rev. Canon Willis). The Canon based his discourse on 'lf God be with us, who can be against us?" (Romans viii. 31). The little word " if" in the text, he said, was not hypothetical; it did not suggest a supposition: it stated a fact. The words could be taken in three senses—firstly as a cry of the soul, secondly as a cry of the Church, and thirdly as a cry. of faith. As applied to the individual it was the cry of con- fidence; of triumphant confidence. When St. Paul wrote the words he was over sixty years of age and had been a Christian for thirty years, so he spoke from experience. He knew what it was to have God on his side. For a thousand years after the Ascension of its Founder the Church of Christ was one, and then—nearly a thousand years ago—that great schism took place between the East and the West—a schism which had caused a breach that had never been healed. Five hundred years later came further separation—a separa- tion which they spoke of as the Reformation. Later still, came other sad separations—the falling away of so many great religious bodies, , as, for instance, the Baptists and the Quakers in the seventeenth century, and the Wesleyans and the Unitarians in the eighteenth. But he thought he saw signs of a healing of the breach. East and West' were approaching each other again. Russia was coming up from the East; great Russia, whose huge army was almost en- tirely composed of members of the Orthodox Eastern Church. Russia thought much of

===== 0002601_19141121_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY'S FALL FROM GRACE.

In the churches of this country they still had reminders of the fact in some of the most beau- tiful of their hymns, as for instance "Jesus lives! No longer now can the powers of death appal us!" "We plough the fields and scatter," "Who are these, like stars appearing?" &c. The German people were Christian people, but the German professors—Nietzsche and others— had thrown over Christianity; thrown over, even, their belief in God; whilst the German Emperor appeared not, only only to have thrown God over, but also to. have taken Him under his wing. Britain had her faults, but, thank God, she also had her virtues. For one thing, she had her methods of government, which were so different from those of Germany. There was no Iron-Heel policy in Britain, but, in- stead, toleration and love, and the belief that everyone should do that he thought well, pro- vided he did right. The aim and object of this .country was to make all happy—not to .make them slaves, but to make them free men— free and happy. And in the present war the cause of this country was a just one—never was a war fought for a more just cause! As a' nation, as a Church, and as individuals they did feel that they had God and the Saviour with them:- Concluding, the preacher said "Our duty is to fight for our country. Thank God! things seem to be going well for us, but the real struggle, I bblieve, is yet to come. 'Twill come when we have driven the Germans back to their Rhine. Then shall we be face to face with the great German fortresses, and we shall need 'more men and still more men,' as Lord Kitchener has said. It is for every man to think 'ls it my duty to go, for the sake of my country, for the sake of my King?"

===== 0002601_19141121_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
THOUSANDS SING THE NATIONAL

ANTHEM.

At the close of the service the procession was re-formed, and, through crowded streets, wended its way back to the Town Hall, where the different units were drawn up in their appointed places on the lawn. The Mayor and other members of the Corporation took up their posi- tion on top of the steps, and at a given signal the massed bands played the National Anthem, the processionists and the large crowd of lookers-on uncovering and joining lustily in singing the grand old hymn. When the last notes of the final appeal had died away, the crowd quietly dispersed, and the aldermen and councillors and other civic dignitaries repaired to the Council Chamber, where, at the invita- tion of his Worship, they partook of refresh- ment, and where the toast of the Mayor and Mayoress was honoured, on the proposition of Alderman BENNETT, who said that in many respects that day had been unique. There had been a splendid processihn, a full church, a beautiful service, and then the final grand assembly on the lawn in front of the Town Hall, a sight which would remain in their memories for a long time. They thanked the Mayor for his public-spirit and the originality which had characterised that Mayor's Sunday.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS WORSHIP AND LORD ROBERTS.

The toast having been honoured, The MAYOR, in reply, thanked all those who had attended, adding: "Will you also allow me to thank you for the very kind manner in which you have toasted the Mayoress and my- self? I am sorry that there is one thing that has marred the proceedings to-day, and that is the death of that venerable and esteemed soldier, Earl Roberts: It was unfortunate that, at his time of life, he should have taken that journey to France, in order to encourage fur- ther the Indian Troops, who have fought so gloriously and so well for this country. (Hear, hear.) He went there to give them that cheer which would do them good." His Worship then' went on to refer to innovations in that day's procession; viz., the engagement of two bands, and also the new feature in shape of the massing of the Friendly Society members and th 4 Pre- paratory Training Corps on the I,awn in front of the Town Hall, remarking that the innova- tion was certainly an unusual one; but, then, the times were unusual and they must do un- usual things.

--- - Mayor's Singleness of Purpose. He might add that anything he did in tbe direction of that day's procession, or in any other connection, he did with a sense of re- sponsibility, and with the thought and hope that he was pleasing everybody and doing his duty as a Mayor should do. It was that feeling, and that feeling only, which prompted him to do what he had done in connection with that day's proceedings, and Ile was glad that the innovatibn—the brief ceremony on the lawn— had met with their approbation. (Applause.) The company then dispersed, and the Mayor's Sunday celebrations were at an end.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR AT WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

ALDERMAN PEACOCK'S PRAISE FOR FRIENDLY SOCIETIES.

There was a most inspiring meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday afternoon, when the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) presided over a large attendance, including members of the various local_ Friendly Societies. The Rev. T. T. James, president of the society, delivered an address, and Miss Elsie Wilkinson earned hearty applause by her fine treatment of the solos " Out of the deep" and "Land of hope and glory," Mr. W. 11. Payton playing the organ accompaniments.

HIS WORSHIP'S AIM AND OBJECT.

Travis read the lesson, al;;;hich

His Worship, who was received with applause. said that he was grateful and gratified to find in the congregation that afternoon so many of his fellow Friendly Society members, especially in view of the bad state of the weather. After their splendid rally of the morning, he had ha,rdly expected so large an attendance. They were all aware that he was a Friendly Society man, and he was trying to fill the posi- tion of Mayor to this ancient borough in a way which would give satisfaction to 'those, of all classes and creeds.

No Friendly Societies in Germany. Friendly Societies had taught him to look at things in a broad manner, and he hoped that his conclusions had generally been right. In the old days, men belonging to such societies used to be put into prison, because they were found meeting in secret; yet now, the Govern- ment welcomed them, for they made men better in all respects. In Germany there were no Friendly Societies. If the people of that coun- try had been allowed to extend their brotherly

===== 0002601_19141121_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
NOVEMBER 21, 1914.

feelings towards mankind more, there would have been no war.

Since the introduction of the Insurance Act, he regretted to say that commercialism had entered into many of their Friendly Societies; however, they were doing all they could to pre- vent it spreading. They had decided that, whilst many of their brethren were away fight- ing, they, would keep them in benefit, so that when they returned they could go on with their membership as if nothing had happened. (Ap- plause.) ' Thanked the Societies for "Practically Everything." He thanked Friendly Societies for practically everything he had, because when he was quite a boy—he did not mind confessing it—he was very, very poor indeed. joining the Friendly Societies when he was 16, brought him some confidence to follow out their motto of asking for promotion when he deserved it. So that now, although State Insurance was with them, he urged men to join the Friendly Societies' voluntary section; they would maintain that " brotherly " feeling, besi&s getting valuable assistance in times of need. Once again he thanked the Friendly Society membeia, without whose help he would not have been so success- ful during the troublous times of his Mayor- alty, and he advised all who could to join. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141121_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
P.S.A. MEMBERS AT THE FRONT

The Rev. T. T. James, in giving the address, alluded to the opportunity they had of showing their sympathy with those who suffered at home as a result of the war, by contributing to the Prince of Wales' Fund. He was very.glad to say that a good many members of that society had gone to the front, and pointed out that the assistance of their dependents was not a charity but a duty. (Hear, hear.) They welcomed their Chairman very heartily; he had presided over many meetings, after which his present task was mere child's play. At least, he would not get into trouble that afternoon. (Laughter.) They wished him all success in his second year of office; his task was a great and difficult one— not so much on account of the multitudinous engagements involved as of the responsibility and anxiety. " Charms " That Grow Off One.

It was wonderful how a town endeared itself to one who lived in it. Before he knew Lanca- shire as well as he did now, he was passing through it, on one occasion in a train, and pres- ently they came—he hoped there was nobody present who lived there=to one of the dirtiest and most ramshackle places he ever saw. And then a man in the carriage, who was returning from Blackpool, jumped up and exclaimed with great fervour: "Good old Wigan!" (Laugh- ter.) Of course, they who lived in Warrington could discuss its shortcomings amongst them- selves, but on no account must they admit them to anybody else. Warrington, A Garden City? - - - -

When his friends came to stay with him he took them from the station up Wilson-Patten- street and Bold-street, and through the Queen's Gardens; and he took them,back past the Town Hall and through the Bank Park. Then he said: " You see, we have quite a garden city !" (Laughter.) But the true value of a town was measured by the lives it contained; and, believ- ing this, they would, on that Municipal Sun- day, go forward and try to make the town better and better. Friendly Societies had done a great deal to inculcate a moral character and ideal. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141121_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED WARRIOR'S HANDSHAKE FOR

THE MAYOR.

Mr. B. Farrar, secretary, also welcomed their Chairman, and referred to his own experiences of the valuable work of the Friendly Societies.

Mr. G. T. Yeates, of Orford, a member of the P.S.A. Society, who had been invalided home from the Aisne, and .was to rejoin his regiment on Thursday, then entered the pulpit and shook hands with the Mayor, Mr. James and the Secretary, amidst applause. All wished him God-speed, and he made a brief, farewell speech.

The collection realised ..tt, making a total two weeks' contribution to the local war funds of £7.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
FOR WARRIORS' WIVES.

Society of Friends Provide a Cosy

Club-Room.

The local members of the Society of Friends have decided to place a comfortable room in their Meeting House, in Buttermarket-street, at the disposal of the wives of the soldiers and sailors who are away fighting their country's battles. Thither the womenfolk may repair for a few quiet hours each day, taking their sewing with them, if they have any to do, or whiling away the time with a chat or a read, for it is hoped that sympathisers with the move- ment will see to it that the tables are liberally stocked with suitable papers and magazines. The room will be opened daily between the hours of three and nine, and a cordial welcome will be extended to the wife of any " Tack " or "Tommy" who cares to avail herself of the facilities offered.

FORMAL OPENING BY THE MAYORESS. This informal club-room-,--to use a description applied to it by a lady speaker—was declared open by the Mayoress (Mrs. Peter Peacock) on Wednesday afternoon, in the Presence of a goodly company, which included a number of soldiers' and sailor? wives.

_ Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes presided over the brief opening ceremony, and, in the course of a short speech, said that the members of the congregation which met in those ftemises had been pondering upon how they could best render assistance to the wives of the men who were engaged in the gigantic struggle which was in progress on the Continent, and, as a result, they had decided to lend that room. There the soldiers' and sailors' wives could meet and spend pleasant afternoons and early evenings, it being assumed that their household duties could be performed in the mornings and later in the evenings. It was to be hoped that those present would show their appreciation by visit- ing the room as often as possible, and also by bringing along their women friends who were situated similarly to themselves. She asked the Mayoress, who, she said, was always good- natured and willing to give a helping hand to deserving objects, to declare the club open. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141121_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
A COUNTER-ATTRACTION TO UNDESIR-

ABLE RESORTS.

The Mayoress, who was greeted with ap- plause, said that amongst the most pleasant of the many duties a mayoress in an important town had to perform was the opening of a social club like the one they were launching that afternoon. The provision of such a comfort- able room for the wives of the brave soldiers and sailors, a room wherein they could encour- age one another and attend to their babies and their sewing during the course of a friendly chat, was a very happy idea, and one which must coffimend itself to all good-thinking people. "We all know too well," continued the Mayoress, "how people talk about where our women spend their time, and it often occurs to me that those people, instead of talking and in many cases scandalising the women, could be better employed by doing fts you have done this afternoon, and by offering inducements which will lead to profit and enjoyment. I have much pleasure in declaring this women's club open." (Applause.)

THE UNDERLYING IDEA. Miss Capper (of Manchester) said the idea underlying the establishment of those clubs was to help, to some extent, to relieve the anxiety which the wives of the soldiers and sailors must feel. A number of similar clubs had been opened in Manchester, and it was to be hoped that similar institutions would spring up all over the country. Mrs. Ransome said that the working arrange- ments were almost complete, and, if that ven- ture was successful, there was every possibility of inaugurating a similar one at the headquar- ters of the Bewsey Adult School. (Applause.) Newspapers and magazines were needed, but thol, it was expected, would be readily forth- coming. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

It Wasn't Al,:

"Johnny," said his mother, severely, "some one has taken a piece of ginger cake out of the pantry." Johnny blushed guiltily. "Oh, Johnny !" she exclaimed, "I didn't think it was in you."

"'lt ain't all," replied Johnny; "part of it's in Elsie."

A MUCH APPRECIATED ACQUISITION._

The Travelling Kaiser. It is semi-officially announced that the Kaiser's headquarters are now in France. His hindquarters were recently seen in Russia.— " Punch."

" Mouchers' " Hard Task. "They had been quarrelling about who was the biggest moucher, and had gone there to fight it out," said Inspector Stuart, at the local Police Court on Tuesday morning, when giving evidence against Thomas Monks, a labourer, of 21, Ellesmere-street, and Robert Heaton, a labourer, of 22, Manley-street, who were charged with fighting in Wharf-street the pre- vious day. The Chief Constable intiniated that the men had set themselves a hard Ulla.— The pugilists were each fined 10s., with alter- natives of 14 days' imprisonment.

The Mayor—" who," Alr./Hallaway informed the Inspector, "has taken 'great interest in the project and practically carried it through him- self "—said the offer of the land to the Cor- poration, at what was considered a very low price, had given general satisfaction in the town. The acquisition of the land would, so to speak, complete the recreation ground scheme for the St: John's Ward, which was simply one mass of congested property, whilst the people of the Fairfield and Howley Wards could get to the ground very easily. Altogether, War- rington was not favourably situated so far as recreation grounds were concerned, and it had been long felt that something should be done in the matter; indeed, for years the Council had been agitated to do something to increase the play-accommodation for the children of the town, and recreation grounds generally. The proposed addition to Victoria Park was easily accessible from all parts. Alderman Bennett said that the acquisition of Victoria Park was regarded as one of the greatest boons ever conferred on the public, and it had been enormously appreciated, as this contemplated addition would also be. There was only one feeling in the town with regard to the acquisition and that was a feeling of unanimous approval.

i

Councillor Archer said he lived only a stone's throw from the Victoria Park. Ratepayers of the ward which he represented had often ex- pressed to him their thankfulness that such. a health resort had been provided. One Sunday evening last summer a matter of no fewer than 4,000 people passed his door on their way to take advantage of the particular spot with which that inquiry was concerned. The inquiry was then closed.
