WARRINGTON'S LUNGS.

Projected Extension of Victoria Park.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
LOAN OF £4,000 SOUGHT.

The Warrington Town Council have asked the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of £4,192 for the purpose of pur- chasing land for the extension of Victoria Park, and on Wednesday the Board sent down one of their Inspectors, Mr. Edgar Dudley, F. 5.1., to inspect the site and go into the de- tails of the application. The Inspector held his inquiry at the Town Hall, when the facts concerning the land which it is proposed to acquire, and what the Council purpose doing with it, were laid before him by the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallaway) and the Borough Surveyor (Mr. A. M. Ker). There were also present at the inquiry the Mayor (Alderman P. Peacock), Alderman ‘43ennett, Councillors Skella.nd and Archer, and Messrs. Piercey (assistant borough surveyor), and Jas. Houghton (chief clerk in the Borough Treas- urer's Department).

===== 0002601_19141121_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THIRTY-SEVEN ACRES OF RECREATION GROUND.

At the outset the Deputy Town Clerk ex- plained that the application was for the pur- pose of getting sanction to a loan for purchasing 37 acres, 3 roods and 15 perches of land, to be used as a recreation ground. Victoria Park, to which the area specified was to form an exten- sion, was purchased by the Corporation under the powers of the Warrington Corporation Act, 1899. The area then acquired was 53 acres, and the price of £lO,OOO included an old manor house, which was still existing. The price worked out at about £2OO per acre, and the land thus acquired—the old Warps Estate—had been used as a public park. The manor house was partly used as a caretaker's residence, but several of the rooms were disused. The house was one of those old buildings that required a lot of money spending on them, if full benefit was to be got out of them, and it had been I suggested that it might be used as an art gal- I lery or something of the sort. Victoria Park, as one looking at its situation would readily appreciate, had been of great advantage as a breathing space, particu- larly to the people residing on the eastern side of the borough. The piece of land which it was now proposed to acquire abutted immediately on to the Park; in fact, a small portion of it—on the south side—jutted right into land already belonging to the town. Warrington was a large manufacturing town, and its manufactures, he was happy to say, were increasing every year, with a consequent increase of population. Under such circumstances sites like the one in ques- tion—seeing that it had a frontage on to a waterway which was navigable for barge traffic —were most suitable for works' purposes.

An Opportunity That May Not Be Repeated. Indeed, there was every likelihood of the whole of the area in the neighbourhood of the land, which it was proposed to acquire, being developed for business purposes within a reason- able period of time. It would be in special demand for works, more particularly as there was a suggestion—which would probably materialise—that the Corporation should put a bridge over the river in this neighbourhood, an enterprise which would open up the whole of the neighbourhood. The Park was situated on the main road, and a footbridge gave access to the other side of the town. The Corporation felt, that in order to round off their boundaries and to preserve for the town—at a time when land was comparatively cheap—a much-needed open space, they ought to acquire this, addition to Victoria Park. If the land which it was now proposed to acquire was opened out for com- mercial purposes and works were put up cheek by jowl with the present recreation ground, it would not be conducive to The best interests of the amenities of the neighbourhood. At present there were in Warrington 93 acres of recreation grounds, but they were scattered all over the town, and some of them Were very small plots indeed. The Inspector: Only three of them are of any size.

No Keep-off-the-Grass Notices. The Deputy Town Clerk said this was the only piece of land the Council had on which the children of the town and the young men could go and play unrestricted and unfettered by any .regulations. In connection with other of the open spaces, there were so many regula- tions—" Beep off the grass," &c.—that children did not really feel "at home." This was a place where they could go and roll about and do just as they liked; that was why the Corpora- tion thought it was absolutely necessary that they should seize the golden opportunity and get the land at the extremely cheap rate at which it was, at present, procurable, viz., about £llO per acre. The land was freehold, but the mine and mineral rights were reserved. There was also a restriction as-to the buildings to be put on it; it being stipulated that the land shall be kept as a park, and that no manufac- turing buildings shall be put thereon, but Only such buildings as are compatible with thkplace being used as a public park.

For the information of the Inspector, the Surveyor went into'the detaili of the plans of the proposed acquisition, explaining that it was intended to use part of the new ground as play- ing fields and part as an ornamental park' though it was not proposed to lay out that latter part at present. As it was at present the land was suitable, without preparation, for plaYing 1 fields. It was the intention, provided it be I found possible, to put in an ornamental lake. but, of course, if it was desired to go on with 1 that scheme, it would, at a later stage, be neces-

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 21, 1914.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WAR AND ITS COST

Mr. Lloyd George's Characteristically Shrewd Budget.

THE GREAT PURPOSE FOR WHICH WE FIGHT.

" Examiner" Interview with Mr. A. H. Grosfield.

"First thoughts on the War Budget?" Mr. Crosfield said: " You will scarcely guess. No, they were not concerned with the huge cost of the war, nor with the manner in which we are to be asked to pay for it. My first thoughts, on reading the Budget, were of a very different character; they were concerned with the won- derful circumstance that this great Constitu- tion of ours should have made it possible for the most stupendous Budget of our time—or of any other time, for the matter of that—to be presented by one who was once just a village lad in Wales. That, to my mind, is one of the things that we should not overlook when we talk about this War Budget; it is indeed a great and inspiring fact. Other thoughts crowd along, but all through the remarkable speech of the Chancellor of the Exchequer the great- ness of his personal achievement compels your, admiration—the wonderful combination of lucidit3c and eloquence, the humour and tact, the courage and simplicity of it all. No wonder I he captivated the whole House—he richly earned that tribute of appreciation." " Then you endorse all that the Chancellor of the Exchequer had to say?" " Why, yes; and, to tell you the truth, I have not yet come across anybody who does not— who does not, at all events, endorse the Budget speech in all its essential features. It would be remarkable if it were otherwise; . the patriotic necessity is so palpable to us all. Of course, it is only on grounds of national neces- sity that taxation so onerous for all @lasses of the community could be defended, and that necessity we all recognise, and are ready, each in his way, to meet. The Chancellor of the Exchequer is quite wise in his decision to make known to us in plain language what the war will cost us, and he has chosen a method which will bring that fact home to everybody, in every class of life. But if the burden of extra taxa- tion is heavy—as it undoubtedly is—l for one am optimist enough to believe that it will not, be laid on the shoulders of our people for any great length of time." " Tell me whence your optimism springs?" I "It springs from „some words in Mr. Lloyd I George's speech:

German Navy will be a thing of the past, and the Austrian Navy, too."

OUR UNCHALLENGEABLE SUPREMACY. "And the British Navy---?" " For us, of course, whether in good times or in bad, the maintenance of our unchallenged and unchallengeable supremacy .at sea is the very breath of our nostrils; it is a vital necessity for our national and imperial existence; but, after all, it is a question of the relative strength of our own Navy, and of other Navies. Japan is our Ally. We have no Chinese Navy to build against. The aborigines of Africa do not build Navies. If our Allies, now co-operating so splendidly with us, are in the mood to re- trench in the interests of one and all of us; what is to prevent us (as I have already asked) from going back a decade, or even two decades, for our standard of naval expenditure?" "You would then drop from the fifty-odd million standard to a twenty-odd million standard?"

"Exactly; it would mean a saving of -froth twenty to thirty millions e year." "But may not the cost of the war swallow all that, and a lot more, before it is ended?" "What the cost of the war will be, none of us know. We have Mr. Lloyd George's estimate that if it lasts a year it will cost 450 millions. Will the war last a year? Well, from the moment the Germans failed in their march on Paris many of us have been sanguine enough to expect, or, at any rate, to hope, that the war would be ended within twelve months. Per- sonally, I never believed—and do not now be- lieve—in the possibility of a two or three years' war, such as some people talk about. The pace is far too hot and the sacrifices too terrible for such a prolongation of the war to be possible. If we continue to strive with might and main to put our full strength into the field, I believe we have good reason to hope that the enemy will be crushed and peace signed before the anniversary of the date when war was de- clared.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
A COMPLETE SETTLEMENT.

"He summed up in one admirable sentence the whole purpose before us and our Allies: 'You have got to settle once for all,' he said, the great questions which have been the cause of irritation in Europe for generations.' You see what is behind that. We have to achieve our object of defeating the enemy so decisively that we shall be able to secure an all-round reduction of armaments on a really large scale. 'I shall regard the war as having failed in one of its chief purposes (Mr. Lloyd George said) unless it led to an all-round reduction in the inflated cost of armaments!' It is in that direction that we must look for an easing of the burdens which this war has thrust upon us. That, however, is for the future. At the moment the thoughts of our people. are con- centrated, and rightly concentrated, in a very different direction."

" You mean that we are straining every nerve to increase and strengthen our lighting capacity?" " Exactly ; and we are doing it; and not for one moment shall we relinquish our efforts till the mighty purpose we have in hand is accom- plished. Somebody said to me to-day: Sup- pose the war should fail to bring about that •blessed result foreshadowed in the Chancellor of the Exchequer's speech!' and my answer is 'Why should it fail?' It is our business to see that it does not fail, and then we shall be in a position to see that never again is the peace of Europe jeopardised, and the wealth of Europe squandered as it has been during the present generation. The frenzied competition in armaments, the increase by leaps and bounds during the last decade or two in the expendi- ture on the Navies of Europe, has led inevitably to this hideous war. Everybody knows who has been responsible for it all. It was Germany that set the pace and forced the pace, and now that the crisis has come we must see to it that the power of Prussian militarism is broken for all time, and that effective measures are taken to safeguard Europe from the arrogant and insolent cruelty of that puffed-up, jack-booted Prussian military caste.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR NAVAL EXPENDITURE.

" That achieved, what is to prevent our hark- ing back to the standard of naval expenditure that served us ten or even twenty years ago? It is in that direction that I look hopefully for means by which we shall lift from the backs of the people the burdens which this inevitable ,War Budget now imposes." "I have heard men say that this war means the end of the movement for a reduction of armaments—that the German attempt to demonstrate that might is right Will compel the nations of Europe in the future to arm to the teeth rather than turn their swords into plough- shares?"

"With such people I do not at all agree. And, mind you, I am not at all what is called a 'Little Navy man.' When I entered the House of Commons in 1906 our naval expendi- ture was, roughly speaking, thirty-one millions. In the succeeding eight and a half years—that is to say, up to the period immediately preced- ing the war—it had actually risen to over fifty millions. During my time in the House of Commons-1906-1909-4 voted for every penny of the increase that was made in those years, and I remember that during the crisis of 1909, when it seemed only too apparent that Germany had been not merely indisposed to respond to the offer we had made in the direction of saner expenditure, but had been trying to steal a march on us-4 remember I had a conversation with Mr. McKenna, who was then at the Admir- alty, a fortnight before he brought in his Esti- mates, and I told him I had absolutely made up my mind to vote every penny he asked for. That is my answer to anybody who may say that my desire to see a reduction in armaments is in any way unpatriotic. Further, if I may be per- mitted another personal reminiscence, I may recall that time after time, in conversation with Lord Haldane, I urged the advisability of more liberal expenditure on our Territorial Force, all, of course, with the view to strength- ening and encouraging it. Those of us who took that line have been well justified in the event for the measures we advised and took to strengthen our position and increase British power on land and sea."

===== 0002601_19141121_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
EUROPE AT ITS WORST.

"And yet you are hopeful of better and saner days to come in the not distant future?" " I am, indeed ; and for this reason: We have an old Lancashire saying, 'When things get to the worst they mend.' Are not things just about at their worst for Europe now? When this war is over, will it not be about time for mending.? What is to prevent us setting about a mending? Not our own people, surely! Does anyone doubt that after the experience of this war our people will not be anxious to put a stop to fruitless and extravagant expenditure in competition on armaments? And what about France and Russia? We have all heard of the heroism and self-sacrifice with which Russia is fighting in this great struggle, but, I do not think our people, as a whole, q'uite grasp the absolutely stupendous scale on which she is conducting her campaign against Germany and Austria (not to mention Turkey). She has a line of battle extending for a thousand miles, from the Baltic to the Carpathians, aid the problem of troops and transports and supplies is on such a vast scale that it means a gigantic cost to the nation. ' Well, remembering that, Russia will not want to throw away money when the war is over. What of France? After the terrible sacrifices which she, like ourselves, is making in blood, and in treasure, she, too, will not want to engage in a race for extravagant armaments. No; whatever else is in doubt as to the outcome of the• war, it is very certain that we need be under no apprehension that either of our ereat Allies is going to set the pace in naval con- struction."

"There is still the enemy to be thought of?" "Oh, aa for that. it is .my belief that .the

===== 0002601_19141121_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
PAYING FROM SAVINGS.

"Now let u-s-as-s-upa-e- that for the interest chargeable on borrowed capital to pay for the war, the improved pay for those who fight our battles, and adequate pensions for those who suffer by them, we have to find something like fifteen millions; against that we shall save—if the hopes raised by the Chancellor of the Exchequer's significant remarks in his Budget speech are realised—something like 25 millions per annum. This is—obviously it can only be —hazarding a rough estimate, but it is well worth bearing in mind at this moment when the burden of new taxation is announced." " You are prepared, then, to relieve posterity of as much as possible of the burden of this war?"

"Certainly; I should advocate that. If my rough estimate were approximately realised we should be making a very handsome present to posterity—such a present as we have never before been in a position to make. It was im- possible to make such a present at the time of the Napoleonic and Crimean Wars. The world had not advanced far enough then. Opinion was not ripe for it then: It will ripen fast before another single year has passed over our heads. This ripening of opinion is an all- important fact which will differentiate our position at the conclusion of this war from the position occupied by our fathers and our great- grandfathers. History, they say, repeats itself —but not always. America had her tremendous Civil War, and it cost her five years of appalling suffering. It will not be repeated; and in all jiuman probability this European Armageddon cannot be repeated either. Given a compre- hensive, thoroughgoing settlement, with due regard to the just claims of nationality and permanent progress, the overwhelming interests of the nations of Europe, big and small, and ,ollective European common-sense will take such steps as will for ever safeguard the nations against a repetition of

Blood, sweat and tear-wrung millions

upon such an appalling scale. That is the glorious legacy which, thanks to the boundless heroism of our men in the field, and the splen- did work of our gallant Allies, we may hope to hand down to posterity. It we do that, it may be asked: Is it reasonable that posterity should be spared a very ample contribution towards prosecuting to a successful issue this greatest of all wars? I cannot doubt that this question was in the mind of the .Chancellor of the Ex- chequer when he reminded us that it is easier to raise taxes in a period of war and to lower them in a period of peace than it would be to raise even lower taxes in a period of peace. But beyond this it is clearly in our present interests to shoulder as much of the burden of the war as possible. Our readiness to tax ourselves for the purpose of paying some part of the bur- den of the war will, as Mr. Lloyd George pointed out, strengthen our credit and give increased confidence that we are not going on by the easiest method of borrowing, but that we are going to carry on the very best traditions of the finances of this country by taxing ourselves for the time being.' Therein is wisdom which may be of advantage to us all, for the maintenance of our credit is the foundation of all our indus- trial prosperity. Mr. Lloyd George, with char- acteristic shrewdness and foresight, has taken care to bring in a War Budget which will not only provide the 'sinews of war,' but which will at the same time nourish and foster, in a time of exceptional stress and difficulty, the trade and commerce on which the well-being of the nation and the Empire depend."

BEER HALFPENNY A GLASS MORE. Local Licensed Nictuallers' Decision.

At a meeting of the Warrington and District Licensed Victuallers' Association held yesterday afternoon it was decided, in view of the new Budget imports, to increase, as from Monday next, the price of,all beers, and stouts, whether on draught or in bottle. A half-penny extra will be charged on each half-pint, a penny on each pint, and twopence on each quart.

Retired Builder's Fortune.

...... - --- The will of the late Mr. Joseph Twiss, of 27, Stafford-road, Warrington, a retired builder, whose death occurred on July 2nd, has been proved at ..e8,858 19s. 10d., the net personalty being sworn at £2,772 48. 3d. The executors appointed are Miss Ruth Twiss, of 27, Stafford- road, one of the deceased's daughters, and Mr. John Shepherd, of 12, Stanley-road, Aspull, near Wigan, grocer. The will is dated October 17th, 1912, and under it, and a codicil, the testator leaves to his wife, Mary Ellen Twiss, the use of his dwelling-house and household effects for life; to his daughter, Lydia Shepherd, house and shop, 6, Rylands-street, for life, and then to her husband and children; 2,4, 6,8, Thelwall- lane, and £3OO to his son, Joseph Twiss, for life on certain trusts. THE SITUATION.

• There is evidently a lull in the South- West of Belgiiim, where, for the moment, the KAISER'S legions have tired of being slaughtered in droves. On our side, we are showing Berlin the kind of determination with which we have entered the struggle, and there is little doubt that before we have gone much further we shall have given quite another interpretation to the. learned German maxim that Might is Right. The right on our side will have attracted so much might to its aid that. might itself will be arrayed *against the unholy alliance that has been raised up against us. In Parliament, Mr. ASQUITII has asked for those mil- lions of men who will fill the ranks till Germany is exhausted; and the Dominions are coming to our assist- ance with feverish enthusiasm. Yes- terday we heard from Melbourne that Australia has promised unlimited men. Financial and other arrangements are being made to enable Australia to send contingent after contingent, as many as are needed, and more than have been asked for. That is the spirit of the whole Empire. Mr. LLOYD GEORGE has arranged to meet the • enormous cost—about £1,000,000 a day at the present time—by moderately taxing tea, beer and incomes, and arranging a war loan, all of which will be cheer- fully met and provided by a resolved People. The re-assembling of Parlia- ment has been notable for the an- nouncement made by the PREMIER that there have been no negotiations about prolonging the life of this Par- liament on account of the war. A week that has shown our people' at their best has been overcast by the death of LORD ROBERTS, while 011 a visit to the front to cheer on the men—as he said about the most useful 'work to which he could at the time put himself. To the end, Mr. ASQUITH said in a noble tribute to his memory, LORD ROBERTS was not only wishing, but working for the success of our arms, and he died where he would have elected to die, almost in sight of the trenches held by his old comrades in arms with the same dauntless heroism which he hirrOelf so often led and inspired. LORD ROBERTS rests, as is fit, in St. Paul's with NELSON and WELLINGTON.

WAR STORIES.

GERMANS KILLED lIIS BLACK " PAL."

Warrington Marine Who Is Longing To Exact Revenge.

Mr. and Mrs. Gamble, of 336. Knutsford-road, Latchford, are a couple deserving of the honour of their fellow-townspeople, for they have given three of their sons to the service of King and country, the third of them having recently gone to the front. In addition three of their sons- in-law are also serving with the colours.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
A SANGUINE SOLDIER.

In a recent letter, one of their sons, Private Arthur Gamble, 41.M.L.1., who is borne on the books of H.M.S. Cumberland, writes that he is glad, in a sense, to hear that his brother Wil- liam is going to the front, adding "but I don't think he will see many Germans, as the war can't last much longer. . . . lam all right, having got over the lot of fighting that fell to our share. They have now put me guarding prisoners . . . . who are aboard their own steamer, which we captured. You can bet they don't get much sympathy from me, as they shot one of my best pals. Although he was a " blacky " he would have laid down his life for me, and I am waiting an opportunity to avenge his death. We are still in the Cameroon Islands, fighting side by side with the French soldiers, who are a good lot and will stop at I nothing.

• SAYS THE GERMAN IS A COWARD. "My idea of the German is that he is a coward Out here one can't sit down and think too deeply, for if he did he would never be able to do what is required of him. Where I am we haven't to care for earth, nor hell! We are not only fighting Germans, but the fever as well. There are only fifty of us Marines here, and half of them are in hospital with the fever. .. . Tell Dad to cheer up. I will boozi be witli lkiga

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

TIIRILLING DRAMA AT TIIE COURT. '

" Anybody's Wife "—a domestic drama con- taining a high percentage of thrills—forms the attraction at the Royal Court Theatre this week, and is being played to good business. The story revolves round the misdeeds of a heartless adventuress, who, by her abominable intriguing, temporarily mars the happiness of a home. There is no lack of incident in the plot, which contains some novel elements, and which is admirably calculated to rivet the attention of all who prefer their theatrical fare served up hot and strong. Mr. Kennedy Allen, who, with Miss Eva Elwes as pen-partner, wrote the play, and who runs the company that is presenting it, has charge of the principal comedy part, that of Silas Slammer, and extracts roars of laughter from his audiences, his drolleries affording a welcome relief amid so much that is pathetic and tragic. A vigorous reading of the part of Bill Blinker—a much-wronged man, who is out for vengeance—is afforded by Mr. Roy Fortescue, and Mr. Fred Ingleby scores heavily as Lord Langley—as undesirable a specimen of theatrical-nobility as ever roused the " Gods" to hisses. The role of the home-wrecking ad- venturess is in the capable hands of Miss Georgie De Lara, who makes the most of all the opportunities afforded her by the authors.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
Big Attraction for Next Week.

The sporting drama, "The Gin-1:-Iler,!' will form the attraction at the Royal Court Theatre next week, and will be presented by Mr. Harry Foxwell's company.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
LADY BOXERS AT THE HIPPODROME.

A genuine and extremely clever exhibition of boxing is given at the Hippodrome this week by four lady champions. The turn is decidedly novel and proves to be popular with Warring- ton audiences. Several exciting rounds are 'fought by Mdlle. Carpentier (champion of the world), Miss Lucie Warner (champion of Eng- land), Miss Adele Neilson (champion of Nor- way and Denmark), and Mdlle. Alice Fleury (champion of Belgium). The ladies display great knowledge of the fistic art, and it is sel- dom that the referee, Mdlle. Jeanette de Lys, has to interfere. A smart ball-punching and club swinging performance is given by Miss Vera Caine. The other turns that go to make up an excellent programme are also very enter- taining and include "The Touch of the Child," a dramatised version of Tom Gallon's story of that name. The playlet is admirably presented by Archie Chappell and Henry Dea's Company. "His Face Is His Fortune!" This old phrase can be used with much truth concerning Ed. E. Ford, the Australian " sundowner," who causes roars of laughter with his comical facial expres- sions.

The "top of the bill" turn at the Hippodrome next week will be provided by the Howard and Collinson Trio, in the screamingly funny sketch, " 'Enery's 'Oliday," whilst another laughable playlet "Jock's Invention, or the Humophone," will be presented by Walter Kemp and Com- pany. Other attractions will be the Kotenichi Troupe in a display of Japanese conjuring; the Eight Varsity Girls, in "Revue Revels"; the Lane and Dale Trio, on their unicycles and bicycles; Lena Bluette, the soprano vocalist; Haydn Mars, "the musical marvel "; and Neville Delmar, with a budget of comic sZings.

STAR KINEMA.

The first of the two splendid programmes at the Star Kinema House this week included "Her 'Really' Mother," a pathetic film, which strongly appealed to the sentiments of the audi- ences. The war news contained in the " Gaumont Graphic" and " Pathe's Animated Gazette" was a very great attraction, and the special, "Our Cavalry's Magnificent Horseman- ship," was one of the most instructive military subjects yet filmed. The house rocked with laughter during the screening of "The Impor- tance of Being Another Man's Wife," with that celebrated comedian, Arthur Roberts, in the chief part. Thrilling adventures were shown in "Three Bags of Silver," an instalment of the serial. "Adventures of Kathlyn," and "A Canine Thief" illustrated a new crooks' ruse.

The second programme, now showing, includes that great naval drama, "Orders Under Seal," and a grand selection of war pictures, which should not be missed. "Sign of the Cross" Next Week. • -- -

A filmed version of the late Wilson Barrett's great play, "The Sign of the Cross," will form the chief attraction at the Star Kinema next week, when special vocal numbers will be ren- dered by Miss Marie Houghion and Madame Raf,Aone.

LICENSEES ON A WATCH COMMITTEE —continued.

Mr. Peacock, as he then was known, was can- didating for Whitecross Ward.

To the Editor of "The Examiner." Sir,—ln your report of the Watch Committee proceedings last week I find that another repre- sentative of "The Trade" has been elected, and this practically by Liberal votes. All honour to Aldermen Tinnion and Pierpoint, and Coun- cillors Locker, Shaw, Wilkinson, Skeriand and Lindley, who courageously -voted against this reactionary proceeding. The following Liberals, viz., the Mayor (Alderman Peacock), Council- lors Dickenson, Halliwell„ Cunnington and Gough, were found in company with four mem- bers of "The Trade" voting for a licensed vic- tualler on this committee, while three Liberals remained neutral, viz., Alderman James Evans, and Councillors Henshall and Barton. A very large number of Liberal electors naturally ex- pect that their own representatives, at any rate, are sound on a question of principle involving, as in this instance, disinterested action on the part of every member of the Watch Committee. This committee exists not only to hear charges against the police, but, as individual members, to prompt the police in the performance of their duty, a considerable ,part of their work having to do with licence-holders. - There are two gentlemen in particular (I refer to Alderman James Evans and Councillor Hen- shall) who, although neutral, puzzle me ,and a number of others. These gentlemen stand before the public of Warrington respected, honoured, and esteemed. We look to such in their public capacity to give a lead on ques- tions where a moral principle is involved, but what do we find? Not only silence of speech, , but failure to vote as well. Some time ago Councillor Henshall successfully contested a seat in the Council held by a publican. He was enthusiastically supported by a number of workers, who put temperance principles in the , forefront of their banner, and now when he has an opportunity to give effect by his vote to their natural expectattons he sits on the fence.' This attitude disheartens everyone whose aim is to free the town from the undue influence of vested interests.

On the general question at election times Liberal candidates invariably tell you that the influence of "The Trade" is unsparingly used to scotch social reform. It is discouraging to see that when they have an opportunity they fail to give effect to their principles.7Yonrs._&c.,

iDETN- GRIFFITITS. Rylanas-street. Warrington, 19th November, 1914.

"Creation of Ideals."

At the Cairo-street Unitarian Chapel on Sun- (l'l3: evening the Rev. Irvine Lister commenced a series of special addresses on the subject of The Influence of Religion upon Society." The first of these dealt with " The Creation of Ileals," and in it Mr. Lister showed that Chris- tian ideals had had, and would continue to have, a tremendous influence on society. In the 91ristian religion there was an ideal for the pdividual, for society, and for the whole world. that for the last-named, he said, was Peace, and the Prince of Peace had given a glorious niDle of peaceful progress and development. -1-t Was in the eternal realities that they should always place their faith.

Music Master's Lecture io Musicians. Mr. W. S. Woods, music master at the War- Vhgton. Training College, gave a lecture on it.tturday afternoon in the Royal Institution, lverPool, before the local members of the In- corporated Society of Musicians, on " The Prac- tical Usefulness of the Tonic Solfa Method to the Professional Musician." The four subjects treated were melody, rhythm (by means of Preach time names), harmony and change of kl,e3*. The points were illustrated by a class of from the Fonthill-road Council School. he lecture was followed with the keenest in- terest, and at the close a very cordial vote of „.thanks to the lecturer was proposed by Dr. C. Reynolds, and seconded by Dr. A. W. Pollitt.

Electric Light for Outlying Districts.

It will be seen from a detailed Parliamentary notice in to-day's Examiner that the Warring- ton Corporation intend to apply in the Session 1915 fOr power to supply electricity to Stockton Heath, Latchford Without, Thelwall, Grappen- hall, Lower Walton, Higher Walton, Appleton, Penketh and Great Sankey. The notice will be read with deep interest by ,the districts con- cerned.

Padgate Wesleyans. A successful meeting in support of Wesleyan Missions was held at the Padgate Wesleyan Church on Thursday evening, Mr. F. Hickman, of Warrington, presiding. The speaker was the Rev. T. H. Wainman, who has had twenty years' experience as a missionary in South Africa, and who went through the Boer War as a chaplain to H.M. Forces. The collection amounted to .t 8 15s. 3d.

Fatal Sequel to an Accident. Whilst following his occupation as a brakes- man on the Dallam clown branch of the London and North-Western Railway line on Monday, Thomas Preston Taylor (57), of 9, Lexden- street, Warrington, was knocked down by a railway waggon, and received such injuries that he died in the Infirmary the same day. At the inquest on Thursday the Coroner's Jury re- turned a verdict of "Accidental death." Mr. J. Longland represented the family of the deceased.

Bowling Club Supper. A goodly company of the members of the King and Queen Bowling Club assembled for their annual hot-pot supper at the hostelry from which the club takes its name, on Thursday evening, when the President (Mr. T. S. Steel) occupied the chair.—Mr. J. Wood, the secretary of the Warrington and District Bowling Asso- ciation, responding to the toast of the associa- tion, said that, despite the troublous times through which the country was passing, the past Beason was a highly successful one from the bowls-man's point of view.—Prizes, of the total value of ..t22, were presented. •

From Soldier to Town Councillor.

It is with regret that we announce the at the age of 67, of Mr. Joseph Shillcock, licen- see of the Mulberry Tree Hotel, Stockton Heath, which sad event occurred on Monday morning. Upon completing a term of service in the Army, Mr. Shillcock became a police officer, first at Halifax and afterwards at War- rington, where he was promoted to sergeant and drill instructor. Upon quitting the force he took the licence of the Bay Horse Hotel, later on moving into the White Hart Hotel, Sankey street, and later still to the Mulberry Tree Hotel. For several years he represented the Town Hall Ward on the Warrington Town Council.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
The Late Mr. Ernest Clayton.

The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Monday afternoon of the late Mr. Ernest Clay- ton, of 13, Orford-avenue, who for 23 years uas connected with Messrs. Arthur Waring and Company's Tannery, Winwick-road. The de- ceased gentleman was for many years a sidesman at St. Peter's Church and actively identified himself with the Young Men's Club in his parish. Friendly Society work also claimed his attention, and he was a popular member of the Orford Cricket Club and the Warrington Bowl- ing Club. Politically he was a Conservative. He passed away on Friday of last week at the early age of 37, leaving a widow and a little daughter to mourn their loss.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
" BOBS" A MODEL ENGLISHMAN.

AVesleyan Minister's Tribute.

A tribute to the memory of the "Grand Old Man of the Army," the late Lord Roberts, was also forthcoming at the annual rally of the ' Wesley Guilds in the Warrington Circuit, held at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Monday evening, when the Rev. W. T. Derry. presided, and, after making a brief reference to Wesley Guild work, said he would like to say a word about that model Englishman—Lord Roberts. Fortunately when a man died his spirit did not die with him, neither did his influence, which, in the case of this great man, would be even stronger now than perhaps it would have been if he had been permitted to remain amongst them. Lord Roberts was one of the first men in the nation. tHear, hear.) He had courage that was indispensable to a soldier, and, although it was common to most of the men in the forces, there was something, an added attri- bute about Lord Roberts, that was not quite so common in the soldier, and that was sympathy and tenderheartedness. That joint attribute was what Lord Roberts possessed in a marked degree; it was a distinguishing feature in his character.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Beloved .By His Men.

It was said that one reason why the British were holding the Germans at bay was because. the German officers treated their men, not as men, but as machines. Lord Roberts never treated his men as machines, and proof of that was found in the affection shown to him by his soldiers. He was certainly tenderhearted, and if a man did make a mistake through his ten- derness of heart it was a mistake which every- one would surely pardon. Another want in the dead soldier's character was his modesty; a thing, which, as a rule, was not to be found in a soldier. He had warned the nation about the great war which was in progress, but when his words came true he was too modest to say "I told you so!" During the last five years 50 per cent. of the men enlisting in the Indian Army were total abstainers. That was chiefly owing to Lord Roberts's influence and example. It would be difficult to find a correspondingly large body of working men in which there was such a per- centage of total abstainers. (Hear, hear.) An- other point in Lord Roberts's character was his splendid patriotism. When Lord Lansdowne sent for him to go to South Africa the grand old Field Marshal said: "For 19 years I have been living temperately and taking regular exercise in order to be fit for such an oppor- tunity as this." Old Warrior's Undaunted Spirit.

" Whether you agree with him or not with regard to his views about National Service," concluded the speaker, "you cannot but admire the undaunted spirit of the old man, who de- voted the last years of his life to bringing before his countrymen the subject which he regarded as of paramount importance—the safety of the Empire! Last week he said: lam going to visit the Indian soldiers. . . . . It seems to be the most useful thing I can do

now ! Lord Roberts had been interesting himself in the soldiers and had been asking for field glasses, &c., for their use. The day had come when he rendered his last service, and he died out in Prance, within sound of the guns, and, as he himself would wish to die, close to many comrades with whom he had served in 'past years. He was a great soldier, he was a great gentleman, and he was •a great Christian. (Applause) A SAD SIGN. _ _

On all the public buildings on Thursday the flags were flown at half-mast, as a token of respect to the great warrior whose remains were interred thai aa:: in St. Paul's Cathedral.

ATTRACTIVE PATRIOTIC FAVOUR.

Mrs. G. S. Pendlebury, 88, Bewsey-road, wife of Mr. Pendlebury, assistant master at the School of Art, designed for her own use, at the outbreak of the war, a patriotic favour, paint- ing on a broad satin ribbon the flags of the five Allies. This was so much admired that she determined to paint others and sell them for the benefit of the War Funds. So great has been the demand that Mrs. Pendlebury has been kept busy producing them ever since, and, although the price is only 3d., she has raised ..t4 for the object named.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
A RED-LETTER DAY.

Mrs. Edelsten reports :—Thursday was a red- letter day in the history of the War Clothing Committee. The ,Borough Treasurer arrived like Father Christmas, laden with parcels, con- taining 26 excellent second-hand suits of cloth- ing, besides 15 pairs of trousers, overcoats, &c. These were received with the greatest joy and dispatched to Fazakerley, as well as a large bundle of shirts, socks, and warm jerseys from St. Paul's Mothers' Meeting and G.F.S.; 2doz. mufflers from the Women's Unionist and Tariff Reform League; and knitted braces and face washers from the Hamilton-street Schools, and gifts from other friends. Letters of thanks were received from the Indian Soldiers' Clothing Fund, and from Major R. D. Willcocks, warmly thanking the commit- tee for their gift of clothing for the hospital ship for the Indian wounded.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
LECTURE FOR RADDON COURT HOSPITAL.

In aid of the funds of the temporary Red Cross Hospital established at Raddon Court, a lecture on "Florence Nightingale" will be given at the Parr Hall, Warrington, on Monday even- ing, December 7th, by Mrs. Ridgway, of Lymm. The lecture will be under the patronage of the Mayor and Mayoress and many other influential ladies and gentlemen. It was at first arranged that the proceeds of the lecture should go to the Belgian Red Cross Fund, but as it was reported in the papers that the Belgian Red Cross Fund had been seized by Germany, it was decided that as Haddon Court was now in use, and a number of Belgians were receiving attention there, to give the money to the Raddon Court Hospital instead. It would have been difficult to choose a more appropriate lecturer than Mrs. Ridgway, and all who are able to do so should seize the oppor- tunity of hearing her upon a subject that will claim her fullest sympathy. Particulars are advertised in to-day's "Examiner."

===== 0002601_19141121_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
VICAR ASKS FOR CHRISTMAS BOXES FOR

" TOMMY."

Writing in the current issue of the Burton - wood Parish Magazine, the Vicar (the Rev. A. M. Mitchell) says:—" We have some forty or more local ` Tommies' mostly in training in the South. ' .... We wish them to know they are not forgotten, not one of them, in prayer and kindly thought. Christmas will enable us to show our appreciation of our young fellow-parishioners in khaki, and to assure them that out of sight they are not out of mind, that we are with them in spirit if far apart in body. Would it not be kind to send each one of OUR khaki-men a little remembrance at the festival of Peace and Goodwill? Some small gift, some small token of affectionate interest sent from the old parish and the old church would do much to cheer the absent soldiers and sailors during a Christmas-tide which must, in any circumstances, be depressing for them. The gift need not take the rather over-done form of chocolate or cigarettes. Some other token'of re- membrance can be easily provided. . • • A suffidiency of money is the only need . . • • a pound or two!" The Vicar adds that a con- tribution box has been placed in the baptistry of St. Michael's for the purpose of receiving subscriptions, which, it is urged, should be made by the first week in December.

FOR THE WAR FUNDS. Successful "Fun Taft" at the Secondary School.

At the Warrington Secondary School on Sat- urday evening was held the fourth scholars' social in aid of the war funds, the arrange- ments this time being in the hands of the Bur- sar Form. There -was a splendid attendance of pupils and parents, and the many entertain- ments provided shadowgraphy, bagatelle, cake's weight guessing competition, fortune- telling, hat-trimming, humorous art gallery, and disc-throwing—were all well patronised. The toffee stall proved a specially strong attrac- tion, running short of supplies at an early hour. A draw for framed engravings of the Prince of Wales and Princess Mary realised the respect- able sum of 295., and the total proceeds amounted to £4 10s.

The teachers and pupils have now collected over•X37 towards the vaious war funds; and, in addition, the girls under the superintendence of the senior mistress have promised to supply 200 pairs of mittens for the local Territorials. They have already forwarded to the Soldiers' and Sailors' Families' Fund a consignment of useful garments; comprising woollen cuffs, Bala- clava helmets, scarves, body-belts, tobacco and cigarettes, and other comforts.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKHOUSE HOSPITAL FOR WOUNDED. •

The committee of the Runcorn Board of Guardians appointed to consider the question of provision for the reception of wounded sol- diers at the hospital reported to the Board on Tuesday that at a meeting they had resolved that steps be taken for making provision for wounded soldiers, subject to the permission of the Local, Government Board and to the War Department being prepared to pay 2s. per head per day for maintenance. The committee in- spected the rooms and resolved that the re- quired number of beds with bedding be pur- chased; that the necessary lavatory accommo- dation be provided; that two anthracite stoves be purchased, and that the Chairman, with Messrs. Morris, Pilling, Mclntyre, Woodward and Spruce, the Master and the Medical Officer, be appointed a sub-committee to complete the arrangements. On the proposition of Mr. Woodward, seconded by Mr. Morris, the committee's recommenda- tions were carried.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
"DISMISSED" COMMITTEE- MEN.

Councillors Resign as Protest.

Councillors H. Roberts and J. G. Craik, who were voted off several important committees last week by the Town Council, have, as a protest, resigned from other committees. They give their decision in letters to the Town Clerk as under:—

===== 0002601_19141121_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR ROBERTS.

Kingsley, Hill Cliffe, Stockton Heath, 16th November, 1914. Dear Mr. Town Clerk,—l am obliged for the notices of the meetings of the various com- mittees upon which I was appointed on 'Mon- day, the 9th instant. In view of the fact that my re-election upon the Watch and Gas Committees was prevented by the reactionaries of the Council I regret that I cannot serve upon any committee except the Education Committee during the current year. The action of the individuals concerned was undoubtedly intended to cause me personal an- noyance, but they have overlooked the fact that they have not only outraged public opinion, but they have also grossly insulted the electors of Whitecross Ward, who will resent-the indignity thus gratuitously imposed upon them in the person of their oldest representative. The Corporation Gas Undertaking, owing to the large amount of ratepayers' capital in- volved, and .the volume of business transacted, requires in its management the help of the best commercial brains and experience which the Council possesses. Ido not think there is a man in Warrington so itted as to suggest that the committee has been strengthened in this important particular by the addition of some, at least, of the individuals who were placed upon it last Monday. The whole circumstances attending the pro- ceedings in question constitute a deliberate breach of the "truce," owing to which the municipal skins of certain members—who would never have been re-elected on their merits— were saved, and is a sorry exhibition of the con- temptible state of mind which enables a certain class of persons in this great crisis of our nation's history, to forget all the decencies of public life.—Yours faithfully, H. ROBERTS. J. Lyon Whittle, Esq., Town Clerk.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR CRAIK. Manehester-road, Warrington,

-November 16th, '1914.

Dear Sir,—As a protest against the disgrace- ful and un-English conduct the members of the Town Council have meted out to me, after 12' years' service, I beg to resign my membership- of all the committees the Council in their "wisdom" thought fit to elect me to serve , upon.—Yours sincerely,

JAS. G. CRAM.

WOUNDED AND A PRISONER.

We understand that Vernon Owen, of the 4th King's Own Lancasters, of 48, Chester-road, is now wounded and a prisoner in Germany. He took part in the Battle of Mons, and answered the roll call on August 25th, but did not respond when his name was called the following day.

FRONT.

Four more members of the local corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade—Corporal Hank in- son, and Privates Looker, Rigby and Tanner— have volunteered for service at the front, and left for Shoreham Camp, Brighton, on Wed- nesday.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED WARRIORS.

The number of wounded soldiers—Belgian and British—under treatment at the Infirmary yes- terday was 37. _ _ _

There arrived at Raddon Court on Saturday eight more wounded soldiers, which brings the number of recuperating warriors now there up to 33-15 Belgian and 18 British. To-day it is expected that seven or eight will leave, and their places will be filled by another batch of the same number, coming from the Western Hospital, Manchester. _ _

On Wednesday afternoon Miss Etta Balmer, of Newton-le-Willows, visited Raddon Court, and kindly entertained the wounded soldiers with a varied musical programme. The songs she rendered in the French language were specially appreciated by our foreign friends.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
STILL MORE RECRUITS WANTED.

During the week 165 men have been accented at Orford Barracks as recruits for Lord Kitch- ener's Army, whilst 70 have offered themselves for service with the Territorials. Under a new arrangement the men who offer themselves are drafted to Blackpool for training immediately upon acceptance, not a day being wasted at home.

More men are urgently needed for Kitchener's Army, especially in Infantry regiments. In- tending recruits—who must stand sft. 3in. or over, and have a minimum chest measurement of 34fin. and must be between the ages of 19 and 38—should apply at the Central Police Buildings, or the Beamont Schools. The Army Ordnance Corps still requires blacksmiths, clerks, foremen, assistant foremen, hammermen, saddlers, storemen and wheelers.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR BELGIAN GUESTS.

Black Bear House, Latchford, in which the local Baptists have undertaken to maintain a family of Belgian refugees, is rapidly being got ready. With the object Of raising a small sum to swell the fund required for the maintenance of the Bold-street hostel of refuge, Mr. A. Clay, the manager of the Liberal Club, gave a duck as the first prize in a draw which took place on Saturday, the fund being benefited to the extent of 16s. as the result of his gift and efforts. Mr. George Miller, wholesale fruit merchant, realised £l7 for the Mayor's Belgian Relief Fund, from the box of fine Belgian pears which was to be seen last week on his stall in the Market. A number of the Belgian refugees who have been staying in Lymrn returned to France last Monday.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
BURTONWOOD MEN AT THE FRONT,

i News is given n the current issue of the Burtonwood Parish Magazine as to the where- abouts and the doings of two of the gallant " Tommies" from the village, who left these shores for France soon after the outbreak of the war. "One, John Fenny," writes the Vicar, "is a prisoner in Germany, which fact ensures his temporary safety. He is allowed to write and receive letters; indeed, he would seem to be kindly treated. "John Riley took part in the battle of Mons, being one of 60 who returned with a whole skin out of a company of 1,200! What carnage! The good Hand of our God was upon our soldier friend, who was, however, wounded and inval- ided home, but has now returned to the front again.. May he enjoy the same good fortune as before—better fortune, coming out of the fiery ordeal unscathed, without scratch or bruise!"

If some people had a strong sense of humour they would die laughing at themselves.

If ignorance were really bliss, there would be far less misery than there is in the world.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
BEER OR COFFEE?

Guardians Discuss Inmates' Christmas Beverage. YULE-TIDE GRANTS FOR OUTDOOR POOR

The Christmas allowances to inmates in the Workhouse constituted the chief matter dis- cussed at the fortnightly meeting of the War- rington Board of Guardians on Wednesday. At a meeting of the House Visiting Committee the Master's estimate for supplies for the next fort- night and for Christmas extras was approved. The extras included a certain allowance of beer to the inmates, and this was taken exception to by three members.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
A DRINK-WRECKED LIFE.

Mr. T. Dale moved as an amendment to the resolution: "That the allowance for intoxicants be deleted from the Christmas fare, and that a substitute which would be equivalent in value be given instead," He did that, he said, for various reasons. He hoped they would not think that the feast would be incomplete if the intoxicants were absent. For a number of years he had assisted in providing a Christmas treat for 600 or 700 persons, and there had not been any intoxicating beverages there, and he ventured to say without fear of contradiction that there was as much joy round that board as round any other board at which sat a simi- lar number of persons. It fell to his lot, on the summons of the Clerk, to make a special visit to the House last week, and to his sur- prise he met an individual whom he had not seen for twenty years. That man had been a slave to intemperance, or he would not have been in the institution that day. His wife and family had turned their backs on him because of his drinking habits. The man had admitted that he was now considerably better than he had been for some time. That was only one type of a number the Guardians had under their control, and he (the speaker) was of the opinion that it would be more cruel than kind to present temptation to men of that description at Christmas time, because he be- lieved it would only stir up an almost uncon- trollable desire for more. He hoped the Board would reconsider the matter. It had been an old custom, hut there was no necessity to con- tinue with it if they thought it was harmful to the inmates -of the House. (Heir, hear.) The Bishop of London declared at a recent meetinr, that lie had been accustomed to being invited" to dinners, and expressed the opinion that two-thirds of those who attended dinner parties took non-intoxicating beverages. The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley): Funny dinners!

Mr. Dale: That is the statement he made, and I believe it. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Hear, hear. The Chairman : •I don't doubt it. The Rev. A. Ist. Mitchell: A splendid man! Mr. Dale: I don't think we should be doing any good to the inmates by offering them in- toxicants after they have been so long under our regime. We should consider this for the sake of the inmates, especially those who are brought here through their intemperate habits. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell seconded the amend- ment, and said he was glad that Mr. Dale had emphasised—though not as much as he might have done—the fact that it was not the wish of total abstainers that the inmates should be deprived of any pleasure or enjoyment. What was proposed was to remove that which was worse and substitute that which was better—to take away the lesser and give the greater. He would suggest coffee and hot milk.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
MISTAKEN KINDNESS.

Miss Broadbent said that she had been un- decided on the subject, but would support the amendment. It was no kindness to the inmates to give them a craving for something which they had been trying to overcome. She did not think anyone could accuse her of trying to de- prive the inmates of their pleasure. Mr. Billinge said he was absent from the House Committee meeting and did not know what the extras were. The Chairman: That the usual amount of beer be allowed! Mr. Billinge : What is the allowance? The Chairman: As in previous years. Mr. Billinge : What is that?

===== 0002601_19141121_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
PINT FOR MEN; GILL FOR WOMEN.

_ Mr. Mather: A pint for the men and a gill for the women. Mr. Billino'n pointed out that non-intoxicants were provided for those who desired them. The inmates had the option of having what they desired. _ _

The Chairman: Mr. Mitchell said he would like to take away the lesser and give the greater. It is like taking a threepenny bit and giving a halfpenny. Rev. A. M. Mitchell: I would make it up in whatever way it could be done. Mr. Mather: After all the strong remarks made, our great man, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, has made-no distinction be- tween tea and ale Let us put them both on the same scale and give the inmates tea or ale, whichever they like. Mr. Ritchie: I propose that they have the beer as usual. I have a glass of beer myself, and I should enjoy my Christmas dinner all the better knowing that the inmates were hav- ing a glass of beer, or a pint. If there is one thing I like better than a glass of beer it is a pint. (Laughter.) Rev. A. M. Mitchell: It is a halfpenny per glass more, you know.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
WORTH THE BUDGET RISE, SAYS A MEMBER.

Mr. Ritchie: I don't care if it is a penny; it is worth it. (Laughter.) These people can have "pop" if they want it. Mr. Hughes supported Mr. Ritchie's proposal. Mr. Bent said that, being in the Trade, he was rather backward in intervening in the dis- cussion of such matters. However, he would be sorry to ,see a repetition of what occurred at the feast last year—one-half of the glasses filled with water and the other half with beer. He had the glasses of water emptied and re-filled with beer, and there was greater rejoicing. It was a great pity to object to those who wanted beer having it.

Mr. Dale: May I be allowed The Chairman: No. On a vote being taken three voted for the amendment, ten against, and two members did not vote.

It was reported that at a meeting of the Padgate Homes Committee the Superintendent's estimate of supplies and ,Christmas extras was approved and the Board confirmed the fiction of the committee.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
EXTRA MONEY FOR OUT-RELIEFERS.

At a later stage in the Board meeting the Rev. A. M. Mitchell moved: "That the extra Christ- mas allowance to out-reliefers granted last year. be renewed this Christmas, viz., adults Is. 6d., and children ninepence," and said that one reason why the allowance should be made again was that the winter had commenced early and promised to be a very severe one. Further than that, there were so many demands that some of them had never got their hands out of their pockets. He thought the Board would not object to the proposal. Mr. Seaton : It is a great pleasure to the poor people in Newton. I second the proposition. Mr. Dale supported, and said that he thought the allowance was properly spent. The out- reliefers required assistance as much as those in the House, for many of them would have been in the House if they had yielded to the pressure which had been brought to bear upon them. _

The Chairman: I opposed . this for .several years, but after the experience of last year I shall not do so. Mr. Billinge : Give the Vicar a chance this time. (Laughter.) The resolution was carried unanimously.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR LOAN.

An advertisement appears in to-day's "Ex- aminer" which will interest investors of all classes. Those who cannot go to the front, but are willing to help with money, have now an op- portunity of contributing to the success of the Allies and at the same time of getting a very good rate of interest for themselves on the best security in the world. The amount asked for is et 350,000,000, at a price which yields 4 per cent. to the investor. If necessary the lists will be kept open until next Tuesday, but those who want to make sure of allotments will go well advised to apply long before then. Any of the banks, money order offices, or principal stock- brokers, will supply the necessary forms. The success of the War Loan is a foregone conclusion, .great enthusiasm having been shown by applicants all over the country.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINK IN WAR 11ME.

Rev. H. Ogden and the Magistrates

and Watch Committee.

PAINFUL STREET SCENES.

At the Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sunday evening the Rev. H. Ogden delivered a pointed sermon upon " War and the Public- house," taking for his text Acts xix. 28: "Great is Diana of the Ephesians."

===== 0002601_19141121_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
SELF-PRESERVATION.

Self-preservation, said the preacher, was the first law of human life. The natural instinct tended to the safety of number one, and was extremely pronounced in the male creature. Drummond, in his " Ascent of Man," said there was another law, warring in their members, which was concerned for the preservation of others. The maternal instinct for the preserva- tion of the race was an absolute necessity, but unfortunately the law for the preservation of self worked in all the schemes and affairs of men. A dog would preserve a shocking collection of bones, burying a hideous heap and gorging himself until his stomach resented more. Man might not gorge his digestion in the same sense, but yet would spoil his appetite for the best things, by his concentration on his own desires and passions and fortunes.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
A MORAL DANGER.

But the public-house was more than a dan- gerous industry; it was a moral danger, and a moral danger, without the question of bread and butter, immediately bound up with it. He would not quote the utterances of any ministers or clergymen that night, for the lay-mind com- monly regarded the ministerial mind as suffer- ing from pious delusions. But such an unimpeachable world-preacher as Tolstoy would better serve their end; and, in one of his letters, lie said: "When a man is sober he is ashamed to piirsue an immoral life; ashamed to steal; ashamed to kill. . . . Thus conscience is deadened by drink." The late surgeon-in- ordinary to Queen Victoria had declared that "alcohol was Satan's chloroform, and that he slayed both body and soul." Judges were rarely fanatics; their utterances were grave and their judgments balanced—as became a judge. Lord Coleridge, in charging the Grand Jury at Swan- sea, took a grave view of the nature of the . calendar of charges before him. A hundred prisoners and an "excessive proportion of sexual character is enough to impress any : judge," he said; and declared that he was al- ' most tired of expressing his opinion that the greater part of the offences were due to drink. In a great many cases the criminal had been supplied with drink in disregard of that section of the licensing laws which said that drink must not be supplied to a person under the influence of drink.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY AUTHORITIES AND DRINK.

The noble Judge had urged the local author- ities to do their duty fearlessly in the preven- tion of crime, and his utterances were a delight- ful commentary upon the Watch , Committee appointments in their own town. Public bodies were not always tactful, and the present time was not a normal time. They needed more strictures upon the public-house in times of peace; in times of war they needed a stiff, stern hand. The soldier and the sailor had to be as fit as any conditions could make them. Lord Fisher had no delusions about war; to him it was pitilessly cruel. That was his method of conducting it, and his instruments and agents had to be fit. Lord Kitchener had pub- lished and re-published his famous order—a German soldier carried his copy of Nietzsche in his knapsack; a British soldier carried his commander's desire for him to forsake drink. Lord Roberts appealed, whilst living, for tem- perance; surely his voice would haunt them from the grave ! Sir John Jellicoe had spoken from the sea, and believed that coffee was more efficient than rum on the quarter-deck and in the fo'castle. Their military and naval leaders were anxious that the men should be St, and fit all the time; and one great agent they believed to be total abstinence.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE WARRINGTON STREETS.

Sir Thomas Barlow pointed out two solutions of the problem—restriction of hours and amel- ioration of conditions. They must, he said, make the local authorities use their- powers, and purify the Watch Committees. These, too often, were made up partly of men interested in the liquor traffic. A good Watch Committee could do an immense amount with the powers they had.

These words were not the utterances of a temperance fanatic, but of the King's physician! The words of Sir Thomas Barlow had been quoted just to emphasise what' could be seen in local streets any night. With more sadness than words could express, one had seen men in His Majesty's uniform leaping and dancing as though mad—rolling along the highway, a menace to themselves and a danger to the pub- lic. One had noticed four pals, with a good deal of chivalry, conducting a helpless pal into the barracks. If a casual observer could see 1 that, what would a systematised search reveal? Patriotism was a word on everybody's lips just then, but was it patriotic to supply drink to a soldier until he was helplessly drunk? Kitch- ener, Roberts and Jellicoe had urged the nation to assist in keeping a sober Army; and the friend who " treated " a soldier already in a state of drunkenness was as bad as a spy. He certainly was a foe in disguise! But what of a trade that was collecting war funds, and assisting in their distribution to the needy, and yet thwarting the wishes of the military leaders? There was certainly a strange irony in their succouring soldiers and sailors, and yet being the head and forefront of a trade that was rendering the soldiers and sailors so unfit.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
"PASSING STRANGE."

It was passing strange that no action had been taken to close public-houses earlier in the town. Sheffield, which was no worse than War- rington from the drink point of view, had decided that no woman should be served with drink before 11.30 in the morning, and that liquor should not he sold after 9.30 in the even- ing, even to bona-fide travellers. It had even taken action, according to report, to make the law applicable to clubs. What was good for Sheffield could not be bad for Warrington. The Chief Constable" had been petitioned by the Anglicans, Roman Catholics and Free Church- men. Their leading representatives had laid their petitions before the Chief; yet, whatever the reason, nothing had been done. One deeply deplored that so beloved a personality as their Mayor could see no reason why the hours of sale for drink should be reduced. The streets, the interior and the exterior of the public- houses were surely the best evidence ,that they needed action—and immediate action!

"TONE" OF THE COUNCIL. But evidently the tone of Warrington's Coun- cil was revealed in their action over the Watch Committee. A councillor of immense value had been displaced, and a seat found on the com- mittee for a licensee. When every progressive town was curtailing the power of the trade, in this borough it must have more room and more power. The Socialists' gibe at the Churches Fag too pointed not to find the weak- ness of their armour. Were Anglicans, Roman Catholics and Free Churchmen not only to write in concert, but also to act in concert, no one would dare to oppose the restriction of the licensing hours. Might the enemies of religion be for .once confounded, and see the churches acting for the rights and benefits of a common humanity!

===== 0002601_19141121_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON JUSTICES' ACTION.

Vicar of Burtonwood's Lament.

Writing in the current issue of his Parish Magazine, the Vicar of Burtonwood (the Rev. A. M. Mitchell) says The Temporary Re- striction (Licensing) ,Bill of August last has practically been nullified in its effects by the provision that the assent and recommendation of the Chief of Police is necessary for the ap- plication of the measure to any particular locality—' and experience,' says a well-known Temperance reformer, proves that the police do not as readily recommend as they 'might, but in some cases have thwarted the local Licensing Committee in their desire to take advantage of the Act.' The Police Chief has proved the crux in a number of districts, but this cannot y said of Warrington, where the magisteria Bench point-blank refuses to curtail the *ll. ities for the sale of intoxicating liquors. Oh, ei Warrington, when wilt thou learn to tread the pathway of sobriety:"
