A DAILY NEED.

after dusk to travel with blinds closely drawn. Those of us who have noticed the effect of this new regulation have been struck by the com- pleteness with which it ensures the invisibility of a moving train the occupants of which have observed it to the letter. Unfortunately, how- ever, this has as yet been achieved but rarely. Regular travellers naturally co-operate with the officials in all such matters and things without question or difficulty; but there are others who are a constant cause of anxiety and troublo owing either to ignorance, carelessness, or down- 'right stupidity, and so, while as a rule the order as to drawing the blinds after dark is being obeyed by most with commendable loyalty and intelligence, there are isolated cases in which it is persistently disregarded, notwith- standing the vigorous remonstrances of station officials and the appeals of guards afraid of being reported. Some of these offenders, who act as if they considered their judgment supe- rior to that of the authorities issuing the in- structions, might possibly change their defiant attitude if they could be shown that otherwise they may personally suffer. Well, they of all in the darkened train offer a mark for any irresponsible maniac who might yield to a" sud- den impulse to take pot-shots at the carriages. I Enough said!

===== 0002601_19141121_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ETERNAL CITY.

Popular Lecture on Rome by a Popular Lecturer.

BLED TO DEATH BY WAR.

Yet another winter, Warrington is having the privilege of a series of popular lectures by Mr. J. E. Phythian—already so well known in the town. This year's course, which is again given under the auspices of the Museum Committee, deals with "Rome, the Eternal City: Its Story and its Monuments," and all the lectures are to be illustrated with excellent lantern slides.

The first of the series—" The Making of Rome and Classical Rome "—was delivered in the Co- operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Saturday even- ing. Mr. John Upson presided over a large audience; and, in a brief speech, stated that a list of library books dealing with Rome had been made out, and the committee would be ade- quately rewarded if they found them in de- mand.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
AIM OF THE tECTURES.

The Lecturer said he had given two series of lectures upon foreign cities before in Warring- ton, viz., on Florence and Venice. The present course had been arranged before the start of the war, and he was glad that he had not been asked to change the subject to one more closely con- nected with the war; for there were some things, in this war, which could only be under- stood if they knew the history of Rome. In these-lectures he would try to say briefly what were the significant achievements of Rome. and so find out, something of the character of the men who played a part in them. To get a know- ledge of what was done in those days, was to get a clue to what was going on to-day. Nobody knew when Rome began to be: lie had seen there the bodies of men who died at least 1000 ir.c.—a fact which could be ascertained from the rate at which soil accumulated. The Romans, themselves thought it was' 753 B.C.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
The Supreme Consideration.

After briefly outlining the old legends as to the foundation of the city, the lecturer went on to speak of its ancient religion. Many virtues, lie said, marked the Romans when they were really at their best. The individual, for in- stance, had to be prepared to sacrifice himself for the good of the community. "None was for a party, But all were for the State." And they not,only had to have all those virtues which might be of service to, them in this re- spect, but they also had to be unsurpassedly good to win the favour of the gods. All this was the real Roman religion, and he believed that they of the present day could do with more of that spirit among them.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
More About World-Power.

The Romans came to think that they were the proper people to rule the whole world. There was a person somewhere on the Conti- nent of Europe—perhaps they would be angry if lie called him a gentleman—(laughter)—who seemed to have the same idea to-day! But the Romans did keep the world in order; and tley ruled it well, for they allowed people to retain their beliefs, and only persecuted the Christians because they did not think them good citizens. Britain had followed the same policy of tolera- tion with regard to the Mohammedans in India, who would not join their co-religionists in Tur- key because they knew their religious prac- tices were safe in the British Empire.

Ca Jeer's Tribute to the Belgians. Describing Ciesar's conquest of Gaul. the lec- turer pointed out that this great soldier-em- peror said the Belgians were the bravest people he had ever had to face. (Applause.) But the Roman Empire fell ! One of the reasons for its decay was undoubtedly the fact that so many of its young men lost their lives in the wars. A war like the present one, he thought, might enfeeble the life of to-day, for in it were perishing many of Europe's finest young men. It had been stated that the French were small because thousands of their finest men were lost in the Napoleonic wars. - That was so in the case of Rome.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
The Forum.

A large number of fine illustrations were thrown on the screen, dealing especially with the arrangement of the great city and its beauti- ful architecture. Referring to a picture of the Forum, Mr. Phythian said that be had stood before these ruilig; and felt that building to have been the milSt wonderful tribute to the P°lver, of the Romans.

Why Britain Rules the Waves.' An officer a the cruiser Undaunted told his friends of his experiences in a recent fight with German destroyers. He was asleep when the Germans were first seen, having been on duty just previous, and his servant knocked at the door and said: ."‘"Beg pardon, sir, four German destroyers have just been sighted. Will you take your bath before or after the action?"

===== 0002601_19141121_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Respecting Aldermen. Certain things said in the vourse of last week's Town Council meeting set me thinking of piti- able displays of human vanity and childishness which I have witnessed in k,onnection with elections of aldermen in various parts of Lan- cashire and Yorkshire. Petty jealousies and heartburnings constantly arise out of such pro- cePlings, and in some cases result in bitter ,uds amounting almost to a sort of vendetta. in one place that I have particularly in mind as I write, the "balance of power" was sum that members of the political party having the slight preponderance, when aspiring to vacant seats on the Aldermanic Bench, were reduced to the humiliating expedient of voting for them- selves, and wondering onlookers were treated to many a sorry exhibition. From what I have ' heard from time to time, that sort of thing has been common enough in different parts of the country. Speaking in the Warrington Chamber on the occasion to which I referred at the out- set, Alderman Tinnion observed, with marked disapproval, that it had been "urged that these honours ought to go round." That is precisely what it came to in one borough where I lived some time. The situation had become so scan- dalous that at last it was arranged that as aldermanships became available, councillors should be elevated automatically according to seniority. The effect may well be imagined. Fancy a big firm promoting their clerks or other employes, not by individual merit, but by a cast-iron rule of that kind! Na, na; it winna dae.

Origin of "Hip, hip—." At the Mayoral luncheon the principal toast was received with musical honours and cheers for the Mayor and the Mayoress. _The explan- ation of the term, "Hip, hip, hurrah !" by-thee bye, has nothing to do with the anatomy. Some time ago a writer in an American paper sent to me by a relative in New York gave it thus : During the stirring times gf the Crusades, the chivalry of Europe was excited to arms by the inflammatory appeals of Peter the Hermit. And while he was preaching the Crusade, this furious zealot was accustomed to exhibit a banner emblazoned with the letters H. E. P., the initials of the Latin words Hierosolyma eat perdita (Jerusalem is destroyed). The people in some of the countries that he visited, not being acquainted with Latin, read and pro- nounced the inscription as one word, "Rep." The followers of the Hermit were accustomed, whenever an unfortunate Jew appeared in the streets, to raise the cry, "Rep, liep, hurrah!" to hunt him down and flash their swords on the defenceless Israelite in token that by his death was typified the result which they desired more than to be charitable. Along with this I set the following from "Chambers' Twentieth Century Dictionary" : "Hip, hip: a cry said to come down from the Crusaders' time, often the cry of the mob in an outrage on the Jews—more probably an abbre- viation of Hebrew than formed from the initials of Hierosolyma est perdita : Jerusalem is destroyed." The conclusion is that the modern exclama- tion, corrupted in its vowel, suggests both Jew I persecution and a bit of Middle Ages fanaticism. Concerning Toasts. To be sure, we have nothing of this sort in mind when, sometimes uproariously and per- haps not very melodiously, but always heartily and sincerely, we unite in chanting "He's a jolly good fellow," and, after formally chal- lenging ,denial which is never forthcoming and is of course no more expected than a forbidding of banns, we proceed to shout ourselves hoarse in the terms quoted in the preceding para- graph. Moreover, we are no longer cynical in our toasts. Many of those that have come down to us from the so-called "good old times," which were really bad old times, are of a de- cidedly cynical character. On a previous occa- sion I gave some examples affecting the ladies which, fair on the surface, had really a bitter sting at the end where the sting is usually to be found. These old toasts, generally put in the form of doggerel, convey the impression of scepticism as to friendship, treating it as a rare jewel and the world as shallow. Even the pretty well-known one "To contentment" is not altogether free from cynicism— "No one bulldog yet could eat Every other bulldog's meat; If you have a good-sized bone, Let the other dog alone." This might be commended to the Kaiser, eh? The Scaremongers. As specimens of this woebegone class have come under my notice, I have referred to their dismal prophecies for my readers' entertain- ment. I have discovered another this week. After the manner of the Fat Boy in "Pickwick," he has been trying to make timid ladies' flesh creep by declaring with emphasis his convic- tion that the Germans will presently be in our very midst. He has made up his mind that they are on the point of invading Englfind by landing their fighting men on the Yorkshire Coast, and that they will march thence to Manchester first thing. one who had appar- ently been impressed by the wondrous tale re- peated it to mile and invited an expression of opinion upon it. I suggested that the alarmist How can the Germans, should be asked—a) already pressed to the limit to their resources, afford to detach from their armies the men and equipment that would be needed for such an enterprise? (2) Even supposing they could spare these, how could they transport them and effect a landing on our shores without first dispcmin„ of our Fleet? When these two questions have been answered there are certain others in re- senre to the benefit of which my perhaps moreperplexed than actually frightened young friend will be welcome.

Precautionary Measures. I take it that a raid by airship is all we have to fear, and that it is for this reason that the railway companies are requiring passengers

TILE

BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

Costly Building Scheme Despite the

War.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding.

NEW CATHOLIC INSTRUCTOR.

At a meeting of the Padgate Homes Commit- tee it was reported that Father Walmsley, the Roman Catholic instructor at the Homes, was leaving the district for St. Andrew's, in Scot- land, and it was decided that, subject to the consent of the Roman Catholic Bishop, the Rev. Father Miller be appointed his successor, at a salary of £lO per annum. It was also recommended that the Superin4 tendent be authorised to engage temporary assistance on the teaching staff whilst Mr. Moly- neux was awv with the South Lancashire Regi- ment.

At the same meeting the Superintendent was instructed to prepare a report showing the num- ber of attendances made to the Homes by the Rev. Ir F. S. Collier since his appointment as chaplain, and also the number of attendances made by the Roman Catholic Instructor during the same period. It was further recommended by the commit- tee that the whole question of the provision of accommodation for children between the -ages of three and five years at the Homes be referred to the original sub-committee appointed to con- sider the Local Government Board Inspector's report.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
LETTER OF CHEER FOR A SOLDIER- SERVANT.

Miss Broadbent said they ought to thank Mr. Molyneux for his patriotism in joining the forces, and she would like them to send a mes- sage from the Board to the effect that they were pleased he was serving his country. Mr. Mather: That is all very well, but there are thousands and thousands who do not get thanks from anyone. He is only doing his duty. The Chairman: Expression of approval is sufficient without a formal vote. Miss Broadbent: That is what I meant. I only •brought it forward because he was one of our officers.

_ Rev. A. M. Mitchell: It is a great encourage- ment to have a message of that sort, and we ought to send it.

ISiiss Broadbent: They ought to get encour- agement and thanks for going. They make great sacrifices.

It Was decided to send a letter.

"THOUSANDS OF POUNDS ON A SCHOOL

FOR TWENTY."

. . Mr. Mather referred to the recommendation with regard to the building accommodation, and said that, in view of the increased prices of materials and the finances of the country, the Board ought to consider whether it was worth while spending thousands of pounds on a school to accommodate twenty children. - The Chairman said he would move that the sub-committee be constituted a committee of the Board to save time in the matter. The Guardians had accepted the scheme and it must be borne in mind that they would not be able to have the children of three and five years in .the present schools after March 31st. The idea of his motion was that the whole thing should be got through on economical lines and without delay. Mr. Brooks seconded and said the new build- ing was only part of the scheme, for there were other things, affecting the administration of the institution to be considered.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said it was because of the Chairman's suggestion that the matter was referred to a sub-committee. A better plan would be to get the sub-committee to report at a special meeting of the Schools Attendance Committee. '

===== 0002601_19141121_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
POSTPONEMENT WOULD BE ADVAN- TAGEOUS. ,

Mr. Billinge : It would be advantageous all round if anything could be 'deferred. . _

INA% A. M. Mitchell:, The sub-committee could report to the next meeting. _ . _

The Chairman: The report ought to be pre- sented this morning if the committee had acted as they should. Mr. Bent: We shall not be doing any build- ing this year. The Chairman: We have not got any plans, and the committee were asked to get them. Mr. Bent thought they should be as economi- cal as they could. _ .

- Mr. Mather: We could not get the building ready by March, it you shifted Heaven and earth.

The Chairman: I don't think we can, but it will show that we intend business. Hey. A. M. Mitchell: There is no reason to daily about it.

Mr. Jolley's motion was carried and the minutes of the committee were confirmed.

THE LATE MASTER SURCHARGED. __

The Clerk reported that the Local Govern- ment Board auditor had summoned Mr. Brown, the late Master of the Workhouse, to appear ,before him the previous day and pointed out to him the letter which he (the Clerk) had sent claim- ing certain sums for food received by Mr. Brown over and above what he was entitled to, and food for his children, to which they were not entitled. The Auditor informed Mr. Brown that he would be surcharged with the whole sum. Mr. Brown promised to forward a cheque the next day. Mr. Billinge : What was the amount? The Clerk: There was £4 65., made up of £3 2s. for chickens and beer supplied to Mr. Brown, and £1 4s. for calves' foot jelly and eggs supplied to the children.

KEEPING HIS SEAT OPEN. The Chairman read a letter from Mr. P. Garnett, who has enlisted in the Sportsmen's Battalion, in which he acknowledged the good wishes of the Board. • In answer to Mr. Mather the Chairman ex- plained that the Standing Orders with refer- ence to a member not attending for six months, and the question of his retirement then, left the action at the discretion of the Board. In the present case the Board would be fully satis- fied.

It was decided, on the proposition of Mr. Mather, to send a letter of encouragement to Mr. Garnett, and assure him, so far as the Guardians were concerned, of his seat when he returned.

Representative Committee Appointed. Several members took part in the discussion, some suggesting that the amount reserved for local use from the sum collected should be 25 per cent.

STOCKTON HEATH

Prompt and Plucky Rescue.—Captain W. Tindall Bell, Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath, saved the life of W. Bramwell, who is employed on the extension of Messrs. J. Fairclough and Sons' mill, on Monday. Bramwell fell off the quay into the river Mersey, and was drifting away, when Capt. Bell divested himself of his coat and vest and dived in. He kept Bramwell floating until a boat came along and picked them up.

Fleeing Before the Germans.—The daughters of Mr. J. H. Fletcher, Holly Dene, Whitefield- road, have received a most interesting letter from a lady who was formerly their companion at Tournai, and who is now at Bethune. The letter recounts many pathetic incidents and stories of refugees leaving their all to escape the Germans. "Many of the poor mothers," says the writer, "were struggling along with their

little ones, who had died-from- cold and hunger." The privations .of niany of the wounded sol- diers are also vividly described, but the writer is very optimistic in her outlook on the war, although she strikes a pathetic note in. the sentence, "We have not heard from father for over six weeks. I wonder if he is a prisoner still." She hoped, however, that she would be able to return to Lens, where she resided before the war, in a few days. -

Training Corps Members at Rifle Practice.— The local members of the Preparatory Train- ing Corps attended Appleton Rifle Range on Saturday for instruction. Mr. A. Shepherd, the musketry instructor, was in charge. Ten shots were fired at a six-inch bull target at 100 yards by each member, five with the miniature rifle and five with the service rifle. The possible 'score was 50 in all. The individual scores were: A. Shepherd, 42; C. J. Smith, 41; W. Skelton, 31; E. G. White, 30; J. Richardson, 30; A. Wil- son, 29; H. Turner, 28; J. Swinton, 28; C. E. B. Sutton, 24; R. Amos, 23; H. Newall, 20; A. S. Wright, 19; W. A. Thompson, 17; S. Rustage, 17; J. W. Robertson, 12; F. G. Pidduck, 10; F. H. G. Semmens, 9; J. Perks, 8; A. Philp, 4. In the evening there was a most successful parade, when about 80 members attended.

Forthcoming War Lecture.—An illustrated, non-party, and educational lecture on "Why we went to war" will be delivered on Friday evening next in the Church Room by Mr. Henry Lancaster (late joint secretary of Lork Kitchener's Recruiting Bureau for East Anglia). Lieut.-Colonel Charles Lyon will preside. The lecture will be interspersed with patilotic and recruiting songs and recitations. There will be a collection in aid of the Belgian Relief Fund. A feature in connection with this lecture is the amicable and harmonious relationship which exists in the parish between the two great poli- tical parties—a relationship which finds expres- sion in the fact that Mr. H. J. Westbrook, the local solicitor and agent of the Conservative party, is collaborating with Mr. S. Albinson, the local agent of the Liberal party. This is surely as it should be at this time.

GREAT SANKEY.

Wesleyan Concert and Conversazione.—On Saturday the members of the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Church held a very successful concert and conversaziofte. The musical portion of the programme was provided by Mr. Levi Richard- son and a party, each of whom rendered their selections with such effect as to secure an encore. Miss Edith Sherburn sang with sweetness and ability "Bird of love divine," &c. Mr. J. Dakin, who possesses a good bass voice, entertained the audience with "Land of hope and glory" (the choir lustily joining in the chorus), and Mr. Richardson himself, a rich, melodious tenor, secured rounds of applause by his ren- dering of "I gave you a rose" (Wright), "At early dawn of day" (Schumann), and several. old ballads. The humorous recitals of Mr. A. Appleton were cleverly rendered, and enthusi- astically receivedi—Prior to -the interval, a pleasing ceremony took place in the presenta- tion to Mrs. Dodds of a framed photograph of tlie organ she has generously given to the church, also of a literary souvenir to Miss Payne in recognition of her earning, for the extension funds, the sum of £3 from a modest penny.—The chair was occupied by the Rev. H. T. Dixon in his usual genial manner, and the ladies of the church helped the funds by the free provision of the refreshments.

FOR THE PRINCE OF WALES' FUND. Committee Appointed to Organise Local Efforts.

The people of Sankey have already given tangible proof of their admiration of our gal- lant soldiers, and of their sympathy for the dis- tressed refugees from Belgium by raiWrtig funds to provide the former with those little comforts that do so much to soften the rigours of cam- paigning,and by helping to feed,clothe and shelter the latter, and now they are turning their atten- tion to swelling the coffers of - the Prince of Wales' Fund. With the object of forming a committee to organise social functions, &c., the proceeds of which shall go to the fund, a special meeting of the Parish Council and a parish meeting were held in the Council Schools on Monday evening.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
Chairman Suggests Ways a-nd _Means.

The Council meeting was presided over by Mr. W. Furness, who said that some time ago he received a letter from the Lord Lieutenant for Lancashire asking what Sankey was doing as a district with the object of organising for getting money for the war fund. He replied that the Rural District Committee had no authority for organising the various parishes, but suggested that the Lord Lieutenant should get the Preston authorities to suggest to 'other committees the advisability of working up Par- ishes, so that each parish could do its level best and get the credit for what was done. He (the chairman) thought that by such means they would get the bet results. That had been suggested to the County Committee, and the Rural District Committee had adopted the sug- gestion and approached the parish councils with the object of getting them to call parish meet- ings and arrange for the organisation of small committees, who would adopt ways and means for raising money for the Prince of Wales' Fund. Teas, hotpot suppers and entertain- ments could be held, and a dozen different things could be clime to get money, without having to take the hat round.

Part of the Money for Local Purposes. Councillor Fair: I think you will get a lot of money that way. Are the proceeds going to the Prince of Wales' Fund, or can we create a local fund?

The Chairman: I suggest that 15 per cent. of the money sent be ear-marked by us for our own committee's special objects and purposes. Councillor Fair: I think there will be a great many objections to the Prince of Wales' Fund. The Chairman said that was a national relief fund, and whilselocally they had had no occa- sion to draw on the fund, there had been enor- mous calls. made upon it from all parts of [the country. There were no people out of employ- ment in Sankey, and the whole of the district was in a healthy condition from the employ- ment standpoint. That was a matter for, con- gratulation. (Hear; hear.) Mr. Smith: It may not remain like that. If the war goes on, say, for two years, we shall have to .draw on the fund. - -

Councillor Fair Ti quite agree with the 15 per cent. being deducted.

Christmas Tea for " Tommy " Suggested.

The Chairman. if our local contingent have a holiday at Christmas we might give them a tea out of that 15 per cent., or else help in the finding of materials for ladies' working parties. There are many channels into which we could divert the money. Mr. Littler, he added, had consented to act as the local treasurer, and Mr. J. Smith was willing to take over the duties of secretary. He had confidence in Sankey, and the public spirit which permeated Sankey, that when occasion required the people would not ! be found wanting either in providing the where- withal, or the time and trouble, to assist all object of the character mentioned.

LYMM.

On the Bench.—The magistrates at the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday were Vice-Chan- cellor Stewart Smith (in the chair). and liressrs. S. Davies, John Coxon, S. W. Gould, H. Holden, T. H. Warburton, and T. J. Ridgway. Yuletide and the Shops.—lt has-been decided that the shops in Lymm will be open all day on the Wednesday in Christmas week, and will close for Christmas Day and Boxing Day. There will be the usual half-holiday on the Wednes- day previous to New Year's Day, whilst the first de: ol 1915 will be regarded as a full holi- day.

Citizen Sunday.—To-morrow will be observed as Citizens' Sunday at Lymm. A special ser- vice will be held in the Parish Church in the afternoon, and will be attended by members of the Urban District Council, the Tradesmen's Association, and representatives of various local societies. A collection will be made in aid of the District Nurse's Fund.

Missionary Sermons and Meeting.—Missionary sermons were preached at the Broomedge Primitive Methodist Church on Sunday by the Rev. R. W. Ferguson, of Lymm. On Wednes- day evening a public meeting was held, when Mr. Ferguson gave the local report, which was of a most encouraging character. Mr. A. B. Edwards, of Lymm presided, and the Rev. G. R. Southall was the principal speaker.

Why the Case was Dismissed.—At the Lymni Petty Sessions on Monday a letter was read from the father of a defendant against whom a summons had been issued for failing to ha v—e a red rear light attached to his car whilst in Warrington-road, Lymm, at 10.30 p.m. on Octo- ber 18th. The letter stated that defendant was serving in the 6th Battalion of the Manchester Territorials, and upon Supt. Sutherland stat- ing that he did not wish to press the case, the summons was dismissed.

Lymm Wednesday F.C.'s Defeat.—The Lymm Wednesday F.C. were defeated on their own ground this week in a Manchester and District Wednesday League engagement by Pendlebury and Swinton F.C. In the first half of the game the home team were unable to break through, but the visitors, showing better form, netted a couple of goals, an advantage they held ;A the interval. On changing ends the local team put plenty of vigour into their attacks u the visitors' citadel, with the result that 11. Jackson and F. Guest equalised the scores. Afterwards the game was even more keenly contested, and it was lot until the last few minutes that a penalty to the visitors enabled them to secure the lead and run out winners by three goals to two.--Next Wednesday the Lymm eleven are due to play the Manchester P. 0., on the latter's ground.

FIVE THOUGHTLESS BOYS.

-- Fined for Throwing Stones at Tele- _ _

graph Foles-:

The small fine which the magistrates at a Juvenile Court, sitting at Lymm on Thursday morning, imposed upon five youthful defend- ants, who were summoned for breaking insula- tors on telegraph poles, must not be taken as indicating the seriousness of the offence. The evidence given to the magistrates (Mr. Alfred Watkin and Mr. J. Holden) showed that six lads went out blackberrying und later corn- menced to throw stones at the telegraph poles which run along the towing-path of the canal at Lymm. Through their acrioa a number of insulators were broken, art' it was !Anted out that in consequence ther3 was the dwnger of important messages being held up. Two of the, boys pleaded guilty and the remainder "not guilty."

===== 0002601_19141121_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
Might Have Delayed War Office Messagss.

Acting-Sergeant Robinson said three of the boys admitted the offence when he called upon them and incriminated the others. All tne boys were of respectable parentage:, • The mothers of some of the boys told the Bench that their lads had been thrashed, whilst others remarked that their sons " had had a good talking to." The Chairman, in imposing a fine of Is. each upon five of the defendants and discharging the sixth, who was only nine years of age, said that the boys had heard of the danger of doing that sort of thing, especially at the present time, when war was in progress. It was possible tllat owing to damage done.by the throwing of stones a message on War Office matters might not be able to be transmitted. That kind of thing must be stopped, and if any of the defendants were brought up again for a similar offence,. or if any other boys were charged with throwing stones at the telegraph poles, the unisment would not be so light.

DE 1R DRINKS.

T‘%o Male-lid i Travellers Fined.

Two casa in which men falsely stated that they were bona-fide travellers in order to obtain refreshments from public:houses during pro- hibited hours, were heard at the.Lymin- Petty Sessions on Wednesday, by Vice-Chancellor Stewart Smith and other magistrates. In the first case taken, Frederick Lockyer, licensee of the Star Inn, Statham, summoned James Bate, a labourer, who resides in Thel- wall-lane, Latchford, for falselY representing himself to be a bona-fide traveller and thereby obtaining intoxicating liquor during prohibited hours on October 23rd. Defendant was also summoned by the police for being found on licensed premises during prohibited hours, and

for giving a false address. Defendant pleaded guilty to all three offences.

Landlord_ Says, "Make An _Example."

Frederick LoclZyer said the defendant visited his premises at 11.45 a.m. on Sunday, October 25th. Witness asked him where he came from, and he replied "Warrington." As the whole of that town is outside the three-mile limit, wit- ness was satisfied that defendant was a bona- fide traveller.

Witness asked the magistrates to make an ex- ample of the defendant in that case, pointing out that it was hard lines on licensees that they should be subjected to such trouble. They tried their best to carry on their businesses in a proper manner, and he thought a stop should be put to the practice of men making false representations in order to get drink. He asked for his expenses..

Sergt. Davies- said that, in company with Acting-Sergeant Robinson, he visited the Star Inn on the morning of October 25th, and found several men on the premises, and took their names and addresses. Defendant gave his name as "James T. Bate," and his address as Buck- ley's-yard, Warrington. This information was found to be incorrect, and it was discovered that defendant was really living within two and a half miles of the hotel.

Defendant, who had nothing to say, was fined ss. on each of the police summonses, and is. and costs on the licensee's summons. The Chairman said that the Bench desired that for the future in cases where the licensee took out the summons first, the police should not take out another summons, or vice-versa. There ought to be. one summons.

gupt. Sutherland stated that the police were the first to take out a summons in this case.

RIGHT _NAME-WRONG ADDRESS.

The second case was against Arthur Ford, a foreman painter, of 15, Rock-road, Latchford, who was summoned by Benjamin George Little, licensee of the Pickering Arms, Thelwall, for making a similar false representation. The police had also taken out summonses against defendant for being found on licensed premises during prohibited hours on October 25th, and for giving a false address. _ _

Mr. Arthur Browne, solicitor, Warrington, appeared on behalf of the licensee, who, he said, kept a book in which he made every per- son who visited the house during prohibited hours sign his name and address. Mr. Little asked defendant where he came from, and re- ceived the reply, "Longford-street, Warring- ton," which was a long way over the three-mile limit, and the entry defendant made in the book in the presence of the licensee, was "A. Ford, 17, Longford-street, Warrington." When the police visited the premises defendant again said that he lived in Longford-street, but inquiries showed that he came from Rock-roaT, which was considerably under three miles away. Con- duct Jike defendants endangered licensees. Even after Mr. Little had gone to the extreme trouble of making everybody sign their names and addresses this defendant gave a false ad- dress.

Benjamin George Little gave evidence bear- ing out his solicitor's statement. Sergt. Davies said that defendant told him that he had written his correct address in the book. However, from inquiries, it was found that defendant did not live in Longford-street, but in a house which was just two miles away. Defendant said that he was taking his eldest boy for a walk on the Sunday morning in ques- tion, and could have called at houses which were over the three-mile limit.

Defendant was fined ss. on each of the police summonses and is. and costs on the licensee's summons.

PENK ETH.

Wesley Guild.—The Wesley Guild held a de- votional meeting in the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Tuesday, when short papers were read by the lady members, and Miss Johnson sang two solos. Mrs. Gunnell presided.

Tannery Bandsmen's Success.—Penketh Tan- nery Band Quartet competed last Saturday in Messrs. Rushworth and Dreaper's Fifth Annual Quartet Contest for Brass Instruments, held in Rushworth Hall, Liverpool. Sixteen sets com- peted for the prizes offered, one being a silver challenge shield. The adjudicator, Mr. Wil- liam Halliwell, of Wigan, commented upon the keenness of the competition, and also upon the high standard of the playing. The result was as follows: 1, Skelmersdale Old; 2, Litherland; 3, Penketh Tannery; 4, Gossage's Soap Works. Messrs. H. Carman, H. Glover, A. Bowyer and D. Massey were the Penketh instrumentalists.

From Waifs to Warriors.—On Monday even- ing at the Friends' School a lectuie on "Dr. Barnardo's Boys in Canada" was given by Mr.l W. Hind Smith. The lecturer first spoke of Dr. Barnardo's life, work and death, and then went on to describe the aetion taken by the boys during the present grave national crisis. The majority of those in the homes, the lecturer said, were too small for military service, but their brass band had been placed at the disposal of the War Office, and had paraded the London streets with the idea of helping further recruit- ing. The band employed a motor 'bus adorned with two striking placards bearing the words, "Free Trips to Berlin and Back, with Plenty to Eat Both Ways." Those lads who were old enough had joined the Army, and Lord Kitchener had been asked if boy buglers were required. Upon an affirmative reply being re- ceived, sixteen of the lads from the homes offered their services and were accepted. A number of Boy Scouts from the homes had also been placed at the disposal of Scotland Yard. Out of 3,000 Canadians who had left the "Land of the Maple Leaf," 400 were Dr. Barnardo's old boys. One of them called at the home in Ply- mouth to say that he was forwarding £1 per month out of his pay towards the home funds,

MANCHESTER • AND DISTRICT

LEAGUE.

RYLANDS' RECS. v. W.EASTE.

-- This match, in the First Division of the above League, was played on the Gorsey-lane enclo- sure before a fair attendance. Rylands' were short of Stretford (their captain), who has enlisted in the local Reserve Battalion of the Terriers," and Warren, who was under sus- pension. Weaste, who won the toss, played the Brothers Brown, of Warrington -" A." Brand- weed kicked off for Rylands', the ball being returned. Weaste took the offensive, but Hor- rocks relieved. Gallop fed his backs well, but Weaste defended soundly. Rylands' forwards, who were playing a grand game, gave the half-- backs every opportunity to open the game, and tries were scored by Povey, Horrocks and Row- linson, Shaw placing one goal. Half-time arrived. with the score: Rylands', one goal three tries kl 4 points); Weaste, nil. At the opening of the second half Weaste kept Rylands' on the defensive, but failed to get over the line. Half-way through this half Rylands' again began to force matters, and Povey, intercepting, raced over, the goal-kick failing. Other tries were scored by Potter, Penn, Horrocks and Rowlinson, the game ending in favour of Rylands' by 1 goal 8 tries (26 points) to nil. Gallop, Povey and Horrocks outshone the Weaste backs, whilst the Rylands' forwards commanded the scrummages. J. Brown, Hard- ing° and Alfleck were the pick of the visitors' team.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF WELL-KNOWN N.U. FORWARD.

Supporters and players alike of the Warring- ton Club—in fact, followers of the "Rugger" code the country over—will hear with regret of the death of that well-known Oldham player, "Bert" Avery, which sad event took place on Saturday, after an illness extending over 22 weeks, the cause of death being that dreaded disease, consumption. Avery was a great favourite with the Wilderspool crowd, and their sympathy will go to the Oldham Club in the great loss they have sustained. Avery, who was only thirty years of age, and came North from Devonport Albion, had been with the Old- ham Club for ten -years, and had done that club yeoman service. His was a record to be proud of. When only 16 years of age he made his first appearance for Devonshire County, when he was playing under the Rugby Union code. He was selected in four International trial games by the Rugby Union, and, in all, played 23 times for Devonshire. Since joining the Oldham Club, Avery, on a number of occasions, has played both for Lancashire and England, and was a member of the first Northern Union combination which was sent out to Australia. At one period he was the record-scoring forward of the Northern Union; indeed, he was a great "emergency" man anywhere on the field, and at different times did duty in every possible position. Avery was born at Buckfastleigh, and stood sft. 104 in., weighing about 14st. He played his last match for Oldham in the con- cluding game of last season, against Barrow.

WARRINGTON AWAY TO-DAY.

Team To Meet Widnes.

Warrington journey to Widnes to-day, and the team to represent them on the Lowerhouse-lane ground was selected on Wednesday night as follows : Jolley. Taberner, Tranter, Brookes, Bradshaw. Daintith, Young. Cot, Clare, Cunliffe, Mather, Critchley, Fairhurst. . .

It will be noted that Green's accustomed place on the forward line is allotted to Fairhurst, the reason being that the first-mentioned player is on the sick list.

"A" TEAM AT HOME. The Warrington "A" team entertain the Widnes second string at Wilderspool this after- noon, and the following team has been chosen to represent the home club: Bramhall; Davies, Sherratt, Steel and Baker; Laws and ,Nicholls; Harmer, Thornton, Logan, Worrall, Fletcher, and Jordan. Both matches will commence at 2.0. This will be the first meeting between War- rington and Widnes this season.

When a woman is abstracted and silent a man always feels guilty; his only doubt is as to what particular thing she has found out.

A man can interest a woman by telling her he is going to be married. But he cannot interest her by telling her that he is married.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 21, 1914.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

Tributes After Forty Years of Poor Law Work.

NO CHANGE IN MEETING DAYS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presiding.

-APPOINTMENTS APPROVED.

. The Local Government Board wrote approv- ing the appointment of Messrs. W. H. War- burton and J. Pearson as relieving officers of the Frodsham and Budworth districts respec- tively, with salaries at the rate of £lOO per annum each. The Board consented to the re- lieving officers holding the offices of registrars of marriages on the understanding that their duties in such capacity would ordinarily be confined to their districts, and subject to their giving a written undertaking to resign the ad- ditional office if at any time the Board or the Guardians should think it desirable. The officers could commence their new duties on the 16th inst. "SEND-OFF" TO AN OLD SERVANT.

The Chairman said they ought to --giN;E: a hearty send-off to Mr. De Prez, who. for the last 40 years had rendered such valuable ser- vices, latterly as relieving officer of the Bud- worth district. The Local Government Board had complimented him on his work. They all wished him long life and prosperity, and a well- earned rest.

Mr. Woodward said he had known Mr. De Prez for 20 years, and he had always found him a loyal and conscientious servant of the Board. He had always shown sympathy with those who were dependent on the Board. Mr. Wright said Mr. De Prez was one of the most perfect relieving officers a Board could have.

Alderman Norman and Messrs. Morris, Spruce, Hallows and Pilling also paid their tributes to the services of Mr. De Prez. Mr. Hallows said Mr. De Prez had always looked after the business of the Board, and at the same time had been sympathetic with the poor.

Mr. Pilling said he had known and admired Mr. De Prez when he was connected with the old Board at Warrington, and also since he came to the Runcorn Board. He was a most honourable and straightforward officer. Mr. De Prez suitably responded, saying that he had tried to do right, and he thought he had succeeded in most cases. PREMATURE'! Mr. Mclntyre was about to offer a welcome to Mr. Pearson as relieving officer for Stockton Heath, when the Chairman mentioned that that matter would come before the Board at the next meeting._

M.P.: IN ACCORD WITH THE BOARD. The Local Government Board wrote formally acknowledging the receipt of a letter of the 4th inst. with reference to the pensions of soldiers and sailors.

Mr. J. F. L. Brunner, M.P., wrote agreeint with the resolution, and said he was pleased to see in the newspapers that details of a pen- sion scheme would be published in a White Paper.

WATCHING OVER THE CHILD-LIFE. The National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children wrote stating that the practical sympathy hitherto shown by the,Board in the work of the Society's local inspector in- duced them to lay before the Board the follow- ing facts: In the course of the past 12 months in this district 166 children had been dealt with, and the supervision visits to their homes numbered 412. The benefits secured to the children, the change in the habits of life of the parents, the improvement of home conditions, affected not only those directly concerned, but brought great advantage to the whole commu- nity. They hoped the Board would again show their appreciation of this work by renewing the contribution which was formerly given. It was decided to make the annual allowance of £2 2s.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
SERVANTS' SUPERANNUATION.

In accordance with notice given, Mr. Thos. Wright moved that a gratuity of £2l be granted to Miss E. E. Cunnah, the late assistant matron at the Workhouse, under the Poor Law 'Officers' Superannuation Act, 1896.

tilt' MOOQn Wati A GRATUITY FOR THE MASTER.

Mr. Morris moved that the sum of £5 be paid to the Master of the Workhouse for services rendered at the Children's Home during the illness of the Superintendent.

Mr. Spruce chairman of the Children's Home Committee) seconded, and the motion was carried. THE MEETINGS "AS USUAL."

Mr. Gibbs moved "that in future, when the meetings of the District Council are not held the meetings of the Children's Home Com- mittee be held at 10.30 a.m.' the House Visit- ing Committee at 11 a.m., and the Board meet- ing at 11.30 a.m." In doing so, he said that that was the unanimous decision of the Board at the last meeting. He hoped that after the experi- ment had been tried the Union would have their meetings at that time every fortnight. Of course, that was a matter for the future, but he hoped they would all Support the resolution before them thlt morning. Alderman Norman expressed the opinion Mat some of the Runcorn members would not be able to get to the Board room in time. After further discussion Mr. Wood said he could not see the necessity for the alteration. The resolution made it practically impossible for the Runcorn members to attend. Mr. Brach moved an amendment to the effect that the Board of Guardians sit at 2.o'clock, and hold the committee meetings beforehand. Mr. Morris: That will penalise us more than ever. .

Mr. Pilling•seconded the amendment. Mr. Gibbs said that on the express wish of the Chairman of the Rural District- Council he wished to withdraw the motion which he had Put forward. It was agreed that Mr. Gibbs be allowed to withdraw his motion. The Board will therefore meet as usual.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
Club Whose Members Have Nearly All Enlisted.

GREAT SANKEY'S AUSPICIOUS START.

Owing to Saturday having previously been set apart for the first round of the Junior Medal Competition, and this having to be postponed on account of insufficient entries, there were not the usual number of matches down for decision in the local League, but of the matches played one of the most interesting was that in which Fairfield United strengthened their position at the head of the League chart by defeating the Central by 3 goals to 1. The victory was well merited, if only for the fact that the United were without the services of Ditchfield, Poniter, Payne and Roberts, who have trans- ferred their services to the Great Sankey Club. _ .

Fairfield Athletic visited' Hollins Green and had to return defeated by five goals to nil. Longford United were disappointed by the non-appearance of the Latchford Baptists' team, and the matter is to be reported to the League Secretary, to be dealt with at the next League meeting.

The Great Sankey Club are to be congratu- lated upon a splendid victory in their first engagement in the Widnes and District Alliance, their opponents being the Ball-o'-Ditton eleven. For a, to all intents and purposes, new team to get the ball through the uprights seven times— four goals being allowed and three disallowed— is a great performance, especially so when the feat is performed on foreign territory. It is all the more to be regretted that the Great Sankey Club were not allowed to play their team in the Second Division of the local League, when it had become impossible to obtain suffi- cient clubs to run the First Division. However, what has happened is that the Warrington League have lost a very good club with a very attractive team of players, who can be relied upon to give a good game and-always command a decent-sized crowd when on opponents' ground. The Widneg\Alliance seized the op- portunity of regaining the membership of the Great Sankey Club, which also means that they will retain their membership for next season as well-1915-16—as time goes a long way ahead. The Stockton Heath Club have also had to Seek re-admission to the North Cheshire League under very similar circumstances to those just mentioned.

Notes by "Linesman.”

Roberts centred from the right and Ditehfield rushed in and headed the ball between the up- rights in splendid style, thus putting the visit- ors two goals up. The Sankeyites continued to have the best of the exchanges, and were all over the home team, whose goal had several narrow escapes. Aided by free kicks the home defenders managed at length to clear their area, but the forwards could not make much impres- sion on the visitors' defenders, and Brindle had little to do. Poulter and Houghton, by smart foot work, led another attack on the home quarters, which ended in .Whitfield scoring the third goal. From the centre kick the home- sters made several raids into the visitors' quar- ters, but they failed to pierce the defence. After a spell of even play in mid-field, the visitors again laid siege to the home goal, and Wigglesworth had to concede a corner. The flag kick was taken by Roberts without result, but shortly afterwards Poulter received the ball and with a grand shot beat the home cus- todian. From this on play was chiefly in favour of the visitors, and the home goal was nearly captured again, the ball just going past the post. The result was: Great Sankey, 4 goals; Ball-o'-Ditton, nil.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

FAIRFIELD UNITED v. WARRINGTON CENTRAL.

These teams met on the ground of the former, the home club having to re-arrange their team owing to the migration of four of their men to the re-constituted Great Sankey Club. The following players .turned out :

Fairfield (fitted : Potter; Cox and Bebbing- ton; Gleave Lewis and Woodhouse; S. Wood- house, Hunter, Scott, Millward and Watkins. Central Hope; Glover and Williams; Goul- den, Brown and Williams; Mason, Hands, Farrill, Emson and Collins. The referee was Mr. B. Howell. The home team won the toss and Scott kicked off before a fair crowd of supporters. Nice combination by Millward and Hunter carried play into the Central goal-area, but Williams relieved with a timely kick. The home team, not to be denied, came again, and Scott opened the score after eight minutes' play. This reverse roused the visitors and they rushed towards the home goal, but finished in very erratic fashion. Gradually the home team gained the advantage, when offside against Scott sent them back. Millward and Watkins put the visitors' goal in danger, and a centre from the latter was promptly netted by Hunter. The Central made efforts to get on terms with their opponents, but were not successful. The half-time score was: United, 2 goals; Central, nil. On resuming, the visitors at once became the aggressors, but Lewis cleared at the critical moment. Watkins, gaining possession, raced away on the left, and, from his centre, Mill- ward was yards wide with his shot. The visi- tors' outside-left was doing well, when Gleave intercepted the ball and transferred. to S. Wood- house, who banged in a grand centre, from which Millward added a goal. The visitors made desperate attempts to open their account, but Emson shot badly when in good position. Goulden, at right half, plied his forwards with good passes, and Mason broke away, and centred well from the touch-line. Farrill was waiting for the ball, and, accepting the oppor- tunity, scored for the Central. Woodhouse tried a long shot at the opposite end, but the ball hit the upright. No further scoring took place, and time was called with the result : Fairfield 'United, 3 goals; Warrington Central, 1 goal.

HOLLINS GREEN V. FAIRFIELD ATHLETIC This match was played 011 the ground of the first-named club, in ideal weather and before a fair muster of spectators, Mr. A. Holt having charge of the game. The visitors' captain had choice of ends, and, straight from the kick-off, the home team assumed aggressive methods, with the result that the visitors' defence was severely tested. Martin was successful in putting on the open- ing point at the end of five minutes' play. Owing to the visitors not being fully represented the home team had much their own way, and Mellor added a second point. After that the visitors showed an improvement, but it was not sufficiently marked to seriously trouble the home defenders, who easily held the upper hand. Martin scored again before the interval, which arrived with the scpre 3-0 in favour of the home players. On the resumption, the visitors defended very well and occasionally tested the home goal- keeper with long shots, which, however, were easily cleared. Hereabouts the home half-back line played very strongly and penned the visi- tors in their own half, a continual bombard- ment being kept up on their goal. Billington scored a fourth goal and Martin added a fifth before the final whistle blew. Result: Hollins Green, 5 goals; Fairfield Athletic, nil.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
WIDNES AND DISTRICT ALLIANCE.

BALL-o'-DITTON V. GREAT SANKEY. The Great Sankey players opened their cam- paign on Saturday in the above competition by paying a visit to the ground of the first-named club. They met with a hearty reception from the spectators. The teams were as follows : itto n Wigglesworth; Currie and Mather; Lamb, Bride and Berry; Brandreth, Yeosley, Mellia, Baldwin and Chorley. Great San key: Brindle; E. Glover and Kelly; J. E. Glover, Millington and Payne; Roberts, Whitfield, Ditchfield, Poulter and Houghton. The referee was Mr. S. G. Essenhigh. The visitors won the toss and Mellia set the ball rolling. After an easy start the visitors settled down to their game, and the home de- fenders were called upon to clear their lines. But they could not hold the visitors, who pres- ently opened their score per Poulter. The home team tried hard to get on equal terms, but in E. Glover and Kelly they met a pair of stout defenders, and were driven back. After Ditchfield had missed the target, the game was evenly contested for a few minutes. The home team gained an advantage from a free kick, but off-side spoiled the effort. Kelly, being at fault, nearly let the home forwards through, but he soon made amends by a fine clearance from the attacking forwards, after which the game was transferred to the home quarters, where Currie and Mather played a fine resolute game and ! kept their lines intact. The home players gradually forced their way to the visitors' end, and tried strenuously to equalise. Off-side against them spoiled another good opening, and at the interval the visitors were still leading by one goal to none. . On changing ends the visitors made the pace and quickly put the homesters on the defensive.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN AND CHESTER LEAGUE.

WESTON POINT B.C. v. BALFOUR-ROAD OLD BOYS.

Played at Weston Point. The visitors won the toss and played with the sun at their backs. The opening exchanges .were very !quiet, and faulty kicking by the visitors' backs allowed the home forwards to get to close quarters, when, following good combination on the home right. C. Prince sent in a beauty, which the visitors' goalkeeper failed to hold, the ball going through off the upright. The visitors attacked, but could not defeat the home backs, being repeat- edly driven back. The home team returning to the attack, Reece, their centre-forward, scored a second goal. Play in mid-field was followed by raids on both goals. Offside against the 'visitors spoilt several good efforts. Grounds notched a third point for the home club. At half-time the score-board read: Bible Class, 3 goals; Old Boys, nil. In the second half .the home team shoved better combination than their opponents, and C. Prince again scored. Even play followed and then the visitors' centre had a splendid opening, but he failed to make use of it. The home team continued to play the better game, and Grounds and A. Reece added further points. Result: Weston Point Bible Class, 6 goals; Balfour-road Old Boys, nil.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
MATCHES THAT DID NOT COME

OFF.

On Saturday the Latchford Baptists should have played Longford United. The latter turned up at the club's headquarters, but they were met by only three of the visiting players, and consequently no match took place. It appears that the Baptists' Committee have been taking drastic measures, owing to the lowly position their club occupies in the League, and with a view to improving their position they have sought to introduce outsiders in the club's playing strength. On the occasion when they gained their first point they had two or three players from Earlestown included in their eleven, and in the team that had been selected to oppose the United they were expecting eight players from Earlestown to figure in their ranks, but they failed to put in an appearance. The committee would do a wise thing to reinstate their own players at once, and thus avoid friction with the Church authorities with which the club is connected. Stockton Heath should have played their first home fixture of the season, but on Friday the secretary received word to say that the Hale- barn Lads, who were to have been their oppo- nents in a North Cheshire League tie, were un- able to fulfil the fixture as practically all their members had joined the Army during the week. This certainly was hard lines on the Stockton Heath Club, as they have played their first three engagements away from home, and were looking forward to this home fixture. The Halebarn Lads made it known three weeks ago that they would probably withdraw from the League. At that time they had not won a match, and their last defeat was 11-0 against them. _ .

Bewsey Wesleyans failed to send a team to fulfil their engagement with the Elmwood Con- gregationalists.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
FRIENDLY MATCHES.

A MATCH "BETWEEN THEMSELVES."

On Saturday the Paddington League eleven , played their reserve team, and much to their surprise were soundly whacked by three goals to one. True, the League team were not at full strength, but, nevertheless, the reserves played stirring football, and their win was by no means a light thing.

BEWSEY AL,BION v. BEWSEY ATHLETIC. These teams met in a friendly encounter on the Arpley ground on Saturday, a good game ending in a draw of.-two all.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
;TEENTII

THE BATTLE OF YPRES.

THE GREATEST BATTLE IN IHSTORY.

The battle of Ypres, which has not yet ended, will rank as the greatest battle in history to date; certainly for Great Britain it is a second and greater Waterloo. Our troops have with- stood the shock of attacks by " droves " of German troops. They served all-comers alik?., and when the Kaiser selected the corpa of giants known as the Prussian Guards, thousands of men, none under six feet high, they anni- hilated the giants as they had annihilated the young students sent against them.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
BAD WEATHER; MEN COMFORTABLE.

Snow has fallen in the northern corner of France. It is not expected that the winter will be severe enough to put a stop to fighting alto- gether. Attacks and counter-attacks will go on as usual, though often under the most trying conditions.

I have talked, says a "Times" correspondent, to a few British soldiers who have been inval- ided back from the fighting line. They are victims of the severe November weather. The overcoats of these men are plastered with mud. They have fought in trenches sodden with rain. Their boots have sunk ankle-deep in the mud; they have stood nearly waist-deep in water. Rheumatism has developed. They are but a few. __

_ Generally speaking, the health of our troops at the front is excellent, notwithstanding the cold and the rain. For this much credit is due to the fine work of the Army Service Corps in the field. Their organisation is wonderfully complete. Their convoys of motor-lorries are continuously at work between the railhead and the trenches.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
A LULL.

An Eye-witness present with General Head- quarters, in a dispatch dated November 16th and issued to the Press on Thursday night, carries on his account of the British operations in South-Western Belgium and Northern France. In a few days following the desperate assaults made on the Ist Army Corps by the Prussian Guard the enemy, although always active, did not attempt any decisive move. Hard and close fighting by British Infantry led to local advances. Eye-witness describes the dar- ing shown by German scouts and spies. The enemy's agents, it would seem, constantly find their way through the British lines to cut the telephone wires from the trenches. ALLIES FIRM. - -

German attacks have been repulsed and the Allies have advanced south-east of Ypres. Several vantage points lost to the enemy some days ago are now once more held by Allied troops. Dixmude, whose capture delighted all Germany last week, has not much benefited the enemy. The inundations that ended the at- tempt to break through on the coast have spread past Dixmude towards Ypres, denying the Germans passage. On Tuesday Paris reported that during the last two days more or less marked progress had been made at every point attacked—at Hetsas on the -Veer, between Armentieres and Arras, in the Vailly region, in the Argonne, and on the heights of the Meuse.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
"MAINTAINED ITS FRONT."

"The British Army maintained its front" was the laconic announcement in the French official communique issued on Wednesday afternoon. A British Press Bureau statement explained what this meant.

The 3rd Division, we were told, was subjected yesterday to a heavy attack, first by artillery and then by infantry, the brunt of both falling upon two battalions of the division. These were shelled out of their trenches, but they recovered them after a brilliant counter- attack, which drove the enemy back in disorder some 500 yards. During the day an attack was also made on a brigade of the Second Division. In this the enemy were repulsed with heavy loss.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
ARTILLERY ACTIVE.

Renewed activity was shown by the German artillery on Thursday along the front in Flan- ders, especially to the north, next the sea. There were no signs, however, of any immediate in- tention of the enemy to renew the infantry attack.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTHERN FRANCE.

GERMAN BOASTING; FRENCH SUCCESSES.

The German Wireless News claims a success in the Argonne.

The French have issued an official statement denying the assertions made by the Germans that the Allies have been driven out of the Argonne Forest. The fighting front of the Allied Army in this quarter has not varied ap- preciably during the past two months. In the Argonne and beyond the Meuse the enemy have suffered several repulses. The French have captured part of a village on the outskirts of St. Mihiel. The village is the only "point d'appui" still held by the enemy on the left bank of the Meuse. The bombardment of Reims by the Germans has been resumed—a sure sign that the enemy have been punished again in that locality. On the left bank of the Meuse, where the Germans hold the village of Chauvoncourt, there has been more fighting, but no decisive result.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAVY GERMAN LOSSES.

The'French communique reports that between the Oise and the Aisne the Germans have been expelled, with heavy losses, from the village of Tracy le Val, which the French troops recently won from them. Further east there was no noteworthy change.

RUSSIAN CAMPAIGN. GERMANS TURN AT BAY. ,

Towards the middle of last week, while the Russians were progressing with their invasion of Poland and inflicting the customary punish- ment upon the Austrians, a "German offensive" was noticed in the direction of Thorn and on both banks of the Vistula towards Wloclawek (20 miles from the frontier), and further to the west. It was ascertained that the enemy had brought up some of the troop ek from Lyck (about 160 miles N.E. of Thorn). Russian troops occupied Tarnow and were gathering to the siege or investment of Cracow. Strong German armies, gathered to prevent an invasion of East Prussia, advanced along both banks of the Vistula. Temporarily at least they were successful. Berlin reports the capture of nearly 30,000 with 70 machine guns. Although both Berlin and Petrograd were sparing of news about events on Germany's eastern frontier, it was clear that between the Vistula and the Warta great forces were in con- flict. Russian official reports, while admitting a withdrawal of the advanced guards operating against the enemy, gave no details. Neither side has yet been broken.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
BERLIN DECORATED:.

—-.- - We were informed, however, via Copenhagen, that on the 17th inst. Berlin was decorated in

===== 0002601_19141121_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====

SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR "THE EXAMINER."

honour of General von Hindenburg's victory. The total Russian losses were estimated at be- tween forty and fifty thousand, and a consider- able number of field cookers were taken.

It was explained by Russia that the Germans had succeeded in concentrating suddenly in one region, that the Russian advance parties had been withdrawn, but that the strategy of their campaign as a whole was unaffected.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
A TEMPORARY CH.ECK.

The repulse of the Russians by General von Hindenburg on the front between the Vistula and the Warta does not appear likely to prove more than a temporary check. The Russian Headquarters Staff on that part of the front reports that the advance guards of our Ally were on Thursday falling back and that the Germans had advanced as far as Piontek.

BELGIUM'S FATE OVERTAKING EAST PRUSSIA.

In East Prussia the Germans are giving way before the invaders, whose progress, although rot rapid, is steady. Many thousand fugitives who have fled from their homes in East Prussia before the threat of a Russian invasion are arriving in Berlin. The Russians have imposed on conquered East Prussian towns war contributions correspond- ing approximately to the war contributions which the Germans imposed on Belgian towns of a corresponding number of inhabitants. The Prussian military authority has ordered all houses to be burned to prevent the Russians from getting shelter.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
CRACOW THREATENED.

Russian and Austrian reports agree in stating that an action on a large scale is developing in the south towards Cracow.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
TURKEY

HAS NOT MADE A GOOD START.

Turkey has not begun the war well. British warships have damaged the forts on the Dar- danelles, at Akaba near the Egyptian frontier, and at Fao on the Shatt-el-Arab in the Persian Gulf. Now the Navy, with military help, has driven the Turks with loss from the defence works at Sheikh Said which watched the further entrance to the Red Sea, while an official an- nouncement concerning navigation OR the Shatt-el-Arab implies that a British expedition has advanced a considerable distance towards Basra. On the Russian frontier things go no better for the Ottoman Empire. Despite vio- lent efforts the Turks have failed to drive the invaders from their territory. East of Erzerum their own cavalry have been vigorously repulsed. An official account of the Sheik Said opera- tions shows they were completely successful. Three battalions of Indian infantry were landed under cover of fire from the cruiser Duke of Edinburgh. The Turks were utterly defeated and most of them captured, together with much material and six field guns. Aden is not far from Sheik Said, which, says the "Manchester Guardian," would provide an excellent base for an attack on the valuable British possession.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
ENTRENCHED POSITION STORMED.

• A smart success has been scored against the Turkish forces on the Shat-el-Arab River in the Persian Gulf. The General in command reports that on November 17th his force stormed an entrenched position, strongly occupied, and took two guns and many prisoners.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
AKABA BOMBARDMENT.

The "Times" correspondent at Cairo reports that the Minerva has again bombarded Govern- ment buildings at Akaba and has landed a party which has driven out a hostile force officered by Germans.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
UNITED STATES CRUISER ATTACKED.

. . A launch belonging to the United States cruiser Tennessee has been fired upon by the Turks while entering the port of Smyrna. The captain has reported the matter to Washington, whence instructions have been sent to the United States Ambassador in Constantinople.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
AFRICA. HEAVY FIGHTING AGAINST ODDS

IN THE EAST.

• Statements on the employment of Indian troops were made in Parliament on Wednesday. Indian soldiers are now doing duty in France and Belgium, Egypt, on the coast near Aden, in East Africa, on the Persian Gulf, and at Tsinc,'- tau. Lord Crewe, who spoke in the Lords, made it plain that the East Alrican position was not satisfactory. When war began the German colony had a much greater military strength than had the neighbouring British possessions. The balance has since been re- dressed, but the fighting has not gone entirely well. During two months the British forces suffered 900 casualties.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
BOTIIA'S PROGRESS.

Official dispatches from South Africa describe operations against sporadic bands of rebels in the Transvaal and Orange Free State provinces. General Botha's work of suppressing the South African rebellion goes on steadily. Beyers, with some 1,500 men, was met by loyal troops in the north-west of the Orange Free Sta,te on Novem- ber 15th. The rebels were attaclted on a front many miles long, put to flight, and pursued for several hours. Rifles, waggons, carts, amrnuni. tion, and horses fell into the loyalists' hands, as did 100 prisoners. A distinctly good effect was produced on the district.

===== 0002601_19141121_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
GOEBEN DAMAGED I PRINCE OF RUSSIAN BLACK SEA SUCCESS. I GOES TO TIIE FRONT AIDE-DE-C.AMP.

The Russian fleet meeting the Goeben and Breslau on Wednesday in the Black Sea attacked them. By use of her high speed the Breslau avoided an engagement, but her super- Dreadnought consort was compelled to fight and was damaged. A series of explosions are stated to have taken place in the Goeben, which took flue.

The Goeben, German built and German manned, was much more powerful a vessel than any of her opponents, of whom the chief, the Sviatoi Evstafi, displaced only 12,000 tons against 22,000, and carried as a main armament only four 12in. guns against her ten llin. pieces.

GERMAN NAVAL LOSSES. CRUISERS INTERNED. I

GOOD NEWS! wE are commencing our 9th Annual Adver- tising Sale To-Day, when we shall offer all our goods at Special Advertising Prices. We should like to state that we have been fortunate enough to have bought all goods for present season before the Declaration of War, thereby saving from 10 to 25 per cent. advance in prices.. We have large stocks of Men's and Ladles' Woollen Underwear at Special Prices, all woollen goods to-day being worth 25 per cent. more than they were la short time ago. All other classes of goods have been well bought, and it will pay you to visit us, see our prices and com- pare qualities. PLEASE NOTE.—We do not buy job special goods for this Advertising Sale, but offer all our good stock, regular numbers, at Special Prices. Our advice to all thoughtful householders is to buy all woollen and linen goods at one.), as prices will be much higher in the early months of 1915. Assuring you that we can give better value than most drapers in the trade, we ask you to come and see for yourselves.

WARRINGTON EQUITABLE INDUSTRIAL CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY LIMITED. Retail' Prices of TEA. We do not carry or purchase heavy Stocks of Tea as it is best retailed Freshly Packed; but there will be NO ADVANCE IN PRICE on the present stocks we hold, but Members can only obtain their usual weekly quantities, The Go-operative Movement is not out to make profit either out of Taxes or necessity, as differences after meeting expenses are returned to its Members as DIVIDEND ON PURCHASES.

LEATHER IS DEARER! Owing to the enormous quantity of Boots required for the Armies of the Allies, practically all of which have to be made in England, there is no possible doubt that Boots are certain to be MUCH DEARER. The Manufacturers demand an increase of 10 per cent. to 20 per cent., and, in certain kinds of Boots, up to even 30 per cent. Since the outbreak of War the price of LEATHER has risen by nearly one-half. It is evident that the Public will have to pay largely-increased prices, or accept an inferior article—one or the other. ) WE ARE, HOWEVER, IN THE FORTUNATE POSITION TO BE ABLE TO OFFER -LARGE STOCKS OF BOOTS, SHOES AND SLIPPERS AT PRICES RULING BEFORE THE WAR. We strongly advise our friends to take advantage of these Stocks, which represent the best possible value. R. ROBERTS & SON 19, BRIDGE STREET.

66 I was so badly disfigured by eczelma A that I was ashamed to be seen,!9 says Mrs. R. A. Smith, of 7, Lamb St., Oldham. "It started with a sore on my upper lip. This sore discharged matter which caused other places to break out. My face, chin, and neck were soon covered with big patches of raw, wet sores which turned nearly black. Then the disease spread to my head and caused my hair to come out in handsful. "A doctor told me I had eczema very badly. As his treatment didn't cure me I attended the Manchester Skin Hospital for some time. The eczema, however, was still as bad as ever. I had been suffering three years now and felt thoroughly disheartened. "Having heard a lot about Zam-Buk I got a box from the chemist. Judge of my delight when, after the first few applications, Zam-Buk began to clear away the painful and disfiguring sores. "The diseased skin peeled as I kept on applying Zam-Buk. New healthy tissue then grew over my face and neck, and my scalp was also thoroughly cleansed of disease. My hair has grown again thick and healthy. "I have lived in this district 22 years, and the splendid cure worked by Zam-Buk has astonished my friends." fr• Zani-Ruk has no equal as a safe and sure cure for eczema, ulcers and festering wounds, poisoned sores, piles, ringworm, etc., and for quickly soothing and healing cuts, scratches, bruises, knocks, etc. NOTE.—Zam-Buk Balm is never sold from door to door. With a view to strengthening their ranks and improving their position in the League, the Elmwood Cons.' Committee have made changes in their team for to-day against Bank Quay Recs., at Lovely-lane. They have included W. Finch in the half-back line. This player should prove an acquisition to the club, as only three seasons ago he was considered of such class that he had several trials with the Everton Club. Another player of promise who has been secured is McKendrick, a centre-forward.

DESERTION CASES. ABSCONDING ARTILLERYMAN. At the Borough Police Court on Monday, John Mills, who had previously been charged with deserting from the 27th Battery of the Royal Field Artillery, was ordered to be handed over to a military escort. ABSENTED HIMSELF OUT OF PIQUE. Edgar Smithson, a private in the 3rd Batta- lion South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Great Crosby, pleaded guilty at the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday to the charge of being an absentee since November 14th.—Mr. T. S. Steel said he was instructed by the family to appear for the prisoner; although, of course, the Bench had no alternative but to hand him over•to an escort. The family desired him to explain that they were very sorry and disgusted at the prisoner's arrest as an absentee. The family was well known in Warrington, and desired to assure the Bench that their brother had not absented himself on any grounds of " funk" or lack of desire to serve his country; lie had been in the Army a number of years, and went through the South African War. Tie was also a member of the National Reserve. He desired to go to the front, and had, on two, occasions, voluntarily offered to go; but his age was against him. There was an idea in the Army, continued Mr. Steel, that, if they did such a foolish thing as prisoner had done, it would help them to get to the front earlier; and he had simply acted out of pique.—Prisoner was remanded to await an escort.
